===PAGE 3=== (plain, 33 lines)
   1| 3
   2| In Memoriam
   3| Jane Doyle was born in Fitchburg on April 17, 1941. She was a
   4| graduate of the Mary A. Burnham School for Girls and a 1963
   5| graduate of Skidmore College with a BSN in Nursing. Jane
   6| earned an MS in Nursing from Boston University and an MBA
   7| in Health Care Administration from Western New England
   8| College in 1984.  Jane was a longtime resident of Lunenburg.
   9| Jane was a member of the National Teachers Association,
  10| National Educational Association, Sigma Theta Tau-Nursing
  11| Honor Society and HealthAlliance Guild, Lunenburg Woman's Club, Lunenburg
  12| Friendly Seniors and the Lunenburg Turkey Hill Family Lions Club. She was the
  13| Chairperson of the Lunenburg Community Council, member of the Lunenburg Council
  14| on Aging, Past-President of the Lunenburg-Townsend Rotary Club, and recipient of the
  15| Paul Harris Fellow Award, a Rotary International Award for Service.  In 2007, Jane
  16| discovered that there was no local food bank in town and coordinated meetings with
  17| fellow Lions’ Club members and Fire Chief Glenny to create a space for residents to drop
  18| off and pick up food.  Her vision lives on today.
  19|     Charles P. Deming, Sr. was a lifelong resident of Lunenburg. Charlie
  20| graduated with the Lunenburg High Class of 1952. He worked as a firefighter for the
  21| Lunenburg Fire Department for 49 years and was an EMT for the first Lunenburg
  22| Rescue Squad. Mr. Deming was employed by General Electric Company for 35 years
  23| and was a member of the GE Credit Union's Board of Directors. He served as a
  24| Lunenburg Cemetery Commissioner. Charlie was treasurer of the 275th Anniversary
  25| Committee and the LHS Athletic Hall of Fame Committee. In 2012, he proudly received
  26| the LHS Alumnus of the Year Award. Charlie coached Lunenburg's Little League
  27| baseball teams, Biddy League basketball teams and was an EMT for Pop Warner
  28| football teams. He was actively involved in many sports and was a member of the GE
  29| Golf League and Lunenburg Men's Softball League. Charlie was known as a "human
  30| GPS"; he could give you directions to anywhere in Massachusetts and knew Lunenburg
  31| like the back of his hand; its highways, back roads, forests, fields and waterways. He
  32| was a talented woodworker and craftsman; family and friends treasure beautiful
  33| reminders of his creativity and generosity.

===PAGE 7=== (plain, 27 lines)
   1| 7
   2| LUNENBURG
   3| Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It  occupies a land area of
   4| approximately 26.4 square miles.  Lunenburg is l ocated in north central Massachusetts
   5| approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
   6| City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
   7| by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
   8| Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting o f all registered voters
   9| in the t own. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeti ng, the affairs of the t own
  10| are generally administere d by a five member Board of S electmen and a  Town Manager. Local
  11| school affairs are administered by an elected seven member school committee while local taxes
  12| are assessed by a three member Board of A ssessors, all appointed on an at -large basis for
  13| staggered three-year terms.
  14| Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies
  15| account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the t own's historical development.  Lake
  16| Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineat ed the Whalom District from the
  17| remainder of Lunenburg.  This district was developed as a housing base for  many factory
  18| workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg.   In many ways the
  19| characteristics of the Whalom District more cl osely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  20| the rest of Lunenb urg. The Hickory Hills area of t own, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  21| developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of
  22| water is Shirley Re servoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  23| that have now been modified to year -round residences. The remainder of Lunenburg which
  24| originally consisted largely of farms and orchards has slowly developed into single family
  25| housing.  Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily
  26| along the two major arteries (Rout es 2A and 13), and the t own's peripheries, due largely to the
  27| close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems.

===PAGE 19=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 19
   2| REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   3| The Town continued to make important progress in 2014 in many areas, including:  School Building; Open Space
   4| Acquisition; Traffic Safety Improvements; Increased Public Transportation; and Organizational Improvements .  In 2014, the
   5| School Building Committee and School Committee brought forth, to Town Meeting, a comprehensive plan for a new Middle
   6| School/ High School Building.  This plan was the culmination of months‟ worth of review, study and public input.  The plan
   7| and project was approved at Town Meeting and by an overwhelming 2 to 1 majority at the ballot.  The building is well under
   8| construction and set to open in September 2016.  Fifty-eight (58%) of the project‟s eligible costs will be paid through a grant
   9| from the MA School Building Authority.
  10| In 2014, both the Conservation Commission and the Open Space Committee prepared and adopted a set of review criteria for
  11| evaluating available land for public ownership.  Each presented its set of criteria to the Board of Selectmen for  acceptance.
  12| The criteria established proved helpful in evaluating two separate Chapter Land Right of First Refusal options during the yea r
  13| and also in supporting the Town‟s application for a Local Acquisitions for Natural Diversity (LAND) grant for the p urchase
  14| of the William H. & Saundra B. Lane Property at Lunenburg Town Forest. This property, about 167 acres in total, provides
  15| critical access to the existing Town Forest at a very affordable price.  Purchase of the property, which occurred in January
  16| 2015, will serve to protect the headwaters of the Mulpus Brook for the long term.
  17| The Town began a comprehensive review of its Traffic Rules and Orders in the fall of 2014.  As a result of this review, it was
  18| determined that a number of existing traffic signs had not been properly authorized, requiring corrective action.  Additionally,
  19| the Board reviewed several different intersections which were called out as being particularly unsafe.  After review, the
  20| Board determined to authorize stop signs at the following locations to improve pedestrian and vehicular safety.
  21|  Flat Hill Road and Sunset Lane
  22|  Highland Street; Main Street and Oak Avenue
  23|  Mulpus Road and Townsend Harbor Road
  24| The Town also continued to work on the redevelopment of Summer Street, a project sl ated to begin in 2016.  In 2014, the
  25| Town committed $230,312 from Chapter 90 Funding to fund the Town‟s portion of the 75% design.  The project is expected
  26| to cost about $7M, all of which will be funded though the Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which is a grant
  27| of State & Federal funds.
  28| One of the most exciting projects is 2014 was the implementation of enhanced public transportation within Lunenburg.  The
  29| Town pays both an annual assessment to the MART and to the MBTA.  The assessment to MART pays for local/ regional
  30| transportation.  The assessment to the MBTA pays for access to the commuter rail system in Fitchburg and Leominster.  The
  31| Town learned through MART that there is a provision in State Law that would allow the Town to use its MB TA Assessment
  32| to purchase additional local transportation, at no additional cost to the taxpayer.  Accordingly, the Town undertook a review
  33| of residents‟ transportation needs and began working with MART to provide additional local transportation.  The addi tional
  34| services are slated to begin in 2015.
  35| One final noteworthy item is the creation of the Land Use Director position which was approved by the 2014 Annual Town
  36| Meeting. The Director is responsible for coordinating the activities of all of the Boards and Departments involved in
  37| regulating land use and land development in town.  The Director will fill the existing role of Planning Director/ Town
  38| Planner, a new role of Economic Development Director, and also serve as a coordinator of the activities of all  Boards and
  39| Departments within the Land Use group.    Additionally, the Director will coordinate with both the Sewer Commission and
  40| Department of Public Works on utility, storm water management, drainage, road extension or development and/ or other
  41| related land development matters.  The Town hired its first Land Use Director, Adam Burney, in September 2014.
  42| I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their time and many
  43| talents to make Lunenburg a better place.  Thank you all for your dedicated service.

   2| TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS

   2| BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   3| The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building at 960
   4| Massachusetts Ave nue. The office hours for the Building Department are Monday and
   5| Wednesday 8:00 A.M. to 4:00 P.M. and Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. to 6:00 P.M., closed
   6| on Friday. Michael J. Sauvageau serves as the Building Commissioner, John Morrealle is the
   7| Assistant Building Inspector and Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant for the
   8| department.
   9| Electrical and Plumbing and Gas permits are now processed by the Building Department.
  10| Building permits for 2014 reflected a 26% increase from the prior year. There were several new
  11| single family homes constructed and while renovations, additions, roofing, decks and siding
  12| permits increased from the past yea r, the greatest increase has been in rooftop solar panel
  13| installation permits. The Emerald Place condominium project continues to move toward the
  14| project completion. The new junior/high school project was permitted in the fall and as of this
  15| report the foundation is in and the steel erection of the building is underway.
  16| The 2009 International Building Code and the 2009 International Residential Code are the
  17| current building codes of the Commonwealth. In addition, as of July 1, 2014, Lunenburg became
  18| a “Stre tch Code” community. Homeowners and contractors are encouraged to review the
  19| requirements of this code prior to permitting.
  20| Carbon monoxide detectors are required by law to be installed in every home regardless of the
  21| age of the dwelling. Also, all swimmin g pools are required to obtain a building permit and an
  22| electrical permit. Inflatable pools that do not have code required fencing around them do not
  23| meet the building code and should be removed and permitted prior to installation.
  24| Residents should be awa re that inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance
  25| requirements.  Please contact our office to determine what step need to take place for safety.
  26| The Commonwealth of Massachusetts Law requires that all person s, partnerships and
  27| corporations who bid or perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alteration, additions,
  28| etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with the Commonwealth.  This office will
  29| enforce this provision; however you should ask you contractor about his/her registration .  Please
  30| contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call any information regarding
  31| zoning regulations or building code issues.

   2| COUNCIL ON AGING
   3| The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   4| supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   5| General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include; “setting policy for the administration of elder programs
   6| or services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.”
   7| The Council on Aging staff consists of; Doreen C. Noble, Director; Susan Doherty, Administrative
   8| Assistant & Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach;  JoAnne Brazell & Ruth Swiecicki, Food
   9| Service Coordinators; Elsa Watson & Judy Alario, Diet Assistants, Gerry Beloin, and James McGuigan.
  10| Board Members are; Cheryl Moisan, Jacquelyn Dwyer, Kevin McNally, Pete Lincoln, Sarah Grant, Betty
  11| DiGiacomo, Diane Nowd, Brain Guenard, Deb, Seeley, Judith Tarbell and Deb Lincoln.
  12| In FY 14 the Council on Aging provided 10,453 units of service to732 participants.  We assisted residents
  13| age sixty and over from the town itself with 62 % of them women, and 38% of them men.
  14| The chart below shows some of the ongoing services that we provide:
  15| Transportation Tax Assistance, SHINE
  16| Congregate Lunch, Coffee Shop, Pancake Breakfast Lunch Bunch Club
  17| Traveling Tooth Fairy, Pet Therapy Days Cribbage, Bridge, Bingo, Wii Bowling
  18| Chair Yoga, Walking club, Stretch & Tone, Line
  19| Dancing
  20| Bocce, Mah Jong, Water Colors
  21| TRIAD, File for Life Meals on Wheels
  22| B/P, Glucose, and Flu Clinics Friendly Visitors
  23| Volunteer Recognition Program Manicure, Pedicures, Reflexology,
  24|  Book Club, Movies, Puzzle Swap Educational, legal, financial &  health related
  25| workshops
  26| Our funding comes from different sources.  In FY 14 we received a total of $121,695 from the municipal
  27| budget for salaries and expenses.  The Montachusett Area Regional Transit Authority (MART) funds the
  28| salaries of our van drivers and half time for our dispatcher.  We also receive funding from the
  29| Massachusetts Department of Elder Affairs.  The Supporters of the Eagle House, Inc. a non-profit 501(c)
  30| 3 organization is the fundraising group for the senior center.  Their contributions are invaluable and they
  31| are ever ready to help us with things that cannot be covered by the municipal budget.  Anyone wishing to
  32| make a donation to aid us in our services to the elderly can make donations to: The Supporters of the
  33| Eagle House, Inc. , Eagle House Senior Center, 25 Memorial Drive. A donation to the Eagle House is a
  34| nice way to honor the memory of a loved one.
  35| Staffing at the Eagle House consisted of a full-time director; one (35 hour a week) administrative
  36| assistant, one (24 hour a week) outreach worker and two food service coordinators who worked a total of
  37| 15 hours a week along with two diet aides who work 8 hours a week.  We also have 2 van drivers who

===PAGE 50=== (plain, 18 lines)
   1| 50
   2| worked a combined total of 30-35 hours a week.   Our staffing levels are minimal for a facility of our size,
   3| our programing levels are extremely high and the amount of services we provide is astronomical,
   4| therefore, volunteers are the backbone of the center, without them many of the services we provide would
   5| not be in place.
   6| Volunteer jobs range from meals drivers, to Friendly visitors, to kitchen assistants, and program
   7| facilitators. In addition we have wonderful people who donate knitted items that are sent to schools,
   8| hospitals, maternity wards and the veterans‟ homes.   Many of the town‟s elderly not only volunteer at the
   9| Eagle House but, also give of their time and talents to the town and other community organizations.  This
  10| year Eagle House volunteers clocked 3000 hours of service.  We also had 11 people participate in the
  11| Senior Tax Work Off program and they provided over 900 hours of labor to various town departments
  12| and the Lunenburg Public School System.
  13| Without the generosity and overwhelming support of the community many of the programs we are able
  14| offer would not be possible.

===PAGE 51=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 51
   2| DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   3| The Department  of Public W orks combines  the resources of the Highway, Park,
   4| Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one
   5| department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to bes t serve the residents of the
   6| community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope of services with a high degree of
   7| efficiency.
   8| DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   9| “Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day , three hundred and
  10| sixty f ive days a year to respond to any weather, infrastructure,  roadway, or  facility related
  11| emergency that may arise.”
  12| We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town,
  13| and ensure others are aware of our contribution. This i s not for an expectation of praise, but
  14| rather to make sure efforts are not forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday
  15| service.
  16| Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at
  17| the table and is a major  player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire
  18| acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of others.
  19| We must continue to build on this awareness.
  20| This department must also reach out to the publ ic we serve and educate them to the fact
  21| that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated
  22| staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid
  23| waste collection and  recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management facilities and
  24| grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are the expected
  25| services supporting everyone in the community.
  26| We will work to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
  27| support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  28| PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  29| The u pdated Pavement Management Plan , inclusive of an electronic database  and a
  30| revised Town-wide pavement defect analysis, identifies the same backlog of work as the original
  31| document established in 2000 . The backlog is now estimated at close to ten million dollars . A
  32| complete survey of every road is underway , but in general , since the level of funding has not
  33| significantly increased, pavement conditions continue to deteriorate more s teadily than the repair
  34| and rehabilitation of Town ways.
  35| Road projects typically extend over two construction seasons and  overlap from one fiscal
  36| year to the next.
  37| Improvements: milling and overlay on a portion of Goodrich St. from Reservoir Rd., top
  38| course on Northfield Rd from Gi lchrest St. to the golf course including asphalt berms to control
  39| storm water, and an overlay on Fish St. to Leominster Rd.  The scope of work  included: a
  40| leveling course of asphalt  installed one season and left over the winter,  followed by a top or
  41| finish course  the next season , sections of “berm” or curbing, shoulder work, minor  drainage
  42| improvements and driveway aprons.
  43| Documentation provided through a “pot hole” log shows that one third of all the available work
  44| days during the season are dedicated to patching and other temporary repairs to similar

===PAGE 52=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 52
   2| roadway failures . As defects  are identified we respond to every location in a timely
   3| manner. This has proven to be  the least effective method of road repair. New pot holes appear
   4| each spring particularly after a severe winter, and often staff revisits the same area year after
   5| year.
   6| The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations however this
   7| application is installed as a temporary solution with an effective life span of around five years.
   8| Many streets that have been “sealed” are well beyond that t ime frame. This process involves the
   9| installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to crac ks or separations in the p avement.
  10| The Pavement  Management Plan also identifies specific areas where this method is the best
  11| option.  Please understand this method does not take the place of curb to curb paving, and is not
  12| applicable in areas of other more serious defects.
  13| The revised  Pavement P lan, no mat ter how complete , is of little value  without an
  14| identified source of funding.  Lack of revenue continues to be the major detriment to improving
  15| the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of f unding is the Massachusetts
  16| Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program.  The estimated value of our 86 miles of
  17| paved streets is by industry standards, calculated to be  62 million dollars. The General Highway
  18| maintenance line within the operational bu dget has remained virtually unchanged  for two
  19| decades while the overall omnibus budget has nearly tripled to  a value in excess of 30 million
  20| dollars. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial inf usion of
  21| funds is necessary.   The conservative estimate remains at  approximately 1.2 million dollars per
  22| year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing allocations.  Applying $400,000 -$500,000 a
  23| year t o a backlog of work that approaches  10 million dollars places this department a nd the
  24| Town as a whole in dismal failure.  Many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major
  25| reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality.
  26| FLEET MAINTENANCE
  27| The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all town vehicles
  28| and equipment excluding Fire Dept. apparatus. High performance police units, park, cemetery,
  29| and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by
  30| department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repa irs has become more frequent  and
  31| therefore more costly . Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all
  32| petroleum based products continues to be a moving target. Applying a solid budget number to
  33| gasoline and diesel fuel costs is ex tremely difficult for Town Officials as well as department
  34| heads.
  35| Throughout this year we installed a vehicle maintenance software program that has
  36| proven to be a valuable tool. Each repa ir is logged by department and vehicle identification
  37| number and mem orialized in a data bank. Every vehicle can be tracked for part numbers,
  38| required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the life of the equipment.

   4| The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to assist
   5| with repairs and maintenance. The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the
   6| need for outsourcin g, specifically preventative maintenance . Staff remains on -call 24 hours a
   7| day, 365 days per year.
   8| Staff keeps buildings compliant and protects the safe running of the work environment. Any
   9| significant interruption of service within Town buildings  carries the potential of disrupting or
  10| disabling necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support staff  are problem
  11| solvers and technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all  of the construction
  12| trades. They deliver services or ov ersee those functions provided by specialized subc ontractors.
  13| Staff manages and forecasts all of the building assets and prioritizes replacement costs.   With the
  14| use of building maintenance software, requests for service are acknowledged immediately,
  15| prioritized, and completed in a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted
  16| upon completion of the tasks a history of the repair is filed electronically.
  17| ENVIRONMENTAL
  18| Storm Water
  19| The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet s et the requirements for the next phase
  20| of storm water regulation and permits. As we await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal
  21| mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
  22| The DPW , as a result of limited funding  is forced to become reacti onary to roadway
  23| drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990‟s earmarked $20,000.00 annually tow ard
  24| rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding
  25| placed the financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively
  26| reducing drainage repairs.
  27| As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are
  28| addressed. Little or no preventative maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done  due
  29| to a lack of available resources.  At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall , drainage
  30| structures in close proximity to open water are cleaned. Other structures throughout the Town are
  31| addressed as time and resources allow.
  32| Waste Water
  33| The DPW co ntinues as the caretak er of the entire sewer system. In an effort to become
  34| more efficient the Sewer Commission has outsourced much of the day to day inspections and
  35| preventative maintenance of the overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations.
  36| Business management and oversight of the subcontractor remain the responsibility of the DPW.
  37| Tree Removal
  38| The infamous “Ice Storm of „08” continues to detrimentally affect trees in the Town right-of-
  39| way. Previously unnoticed damage together with dying limbs creates a backlog of tree felling
  40| and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each resident
  41| request and must prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and
  42| property. A more efficient approach would involve a survey of all Town owned trees with a
  43| generated list of those requiring attention. Until a plan is in place, the DPW will act in a

===PAGE 54=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 54
   2| reactionary mode. All work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small
   3| diameter trees is outsourced due to the required use of a bucket truck and a licensed professional.
   4| Each spring DPW staff moves Town -wide removing and disposing of woody debris within the
   5| right-of-way.
   6| Landfill
   7| The “capped” landfill off Young‟ s R oad is in compli ance with the Division of
   8| Environmental Protection. The site is monitored  regularly for excessive methane gas as well as
   9| the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  10| The frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result o f
  11| consistently favorable results.  The landfill site including the  perimeter fencing is inspected on a
  12| regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  13| Adjacent to the “cap” is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings ,
  14| both determined by the EPA  to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials
  15| through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires
  16| yearly testing of these materials.
  17| On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage
  18| the annual composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to
  19| our residents and add a valuable component to our recycling program.  There has to date been no
  20| dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again
  21| dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to maintain  this location while keeping it
  22| environmentally safe.
  23| Solid Waste
  24| Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to fac ilitate the
  25| curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc.
  26| Lake Shirley Dam
  27| In the early 1990‟s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley
  28| Association turned over ownership of the dam to the Town. A fter completion of a total
  29| reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed
  30| solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association . In 2004 -2005 custodianship
  31| of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as
  32| well as an emergency action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department
  33| of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance
  34| with current and fu ture regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  35| professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection
  36| resulted in a positive report.
  37| WINTER OPERATIONS
  38| Snow and ice present a major challenge to our t own. Each Town department and the
  39| safety of the general public depend on the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe
  40| condition despite the severity or the duration of snow and ice events. To that end we develop a
  41| winter operations procedure based upon a well thought out plan that must be flexible due to the
  42| unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is expected and must be
  43| delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.

===PAGE 55=== (plain, 33 lines)
   1| 55
   2| Preparations begin with successful imp lementation of the Capital P lan. Rotation of the
   3| fleet in order to reduce breakdowns and minimize repairs is contingent upon resident support.
   4| Many of the front line vehicles are well beyond the optimum time for replacement. In the fall salt
   5| and sand are delivered to the Chase Road  facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other
   6| communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for salt. Individually the Town also
   7| solicits bids for road sand. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and
   8| prepares the trucks, sanders and plows for winter operations. During the construction season road
   9| crews address right -of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to
  10| eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations.
  11| Typically DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  12| Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the
  13| overall cost. For example, a fast moving s now storm of 6 -8 inches will cost significantly less
  14| than a long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically,
  15| the department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average
  16| winter.
  17| PARKS
  18| In conjunction with the three ele cted Park Commissioners , DPW staff  oversees and
  19| maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school pro perty. A full time Park employee  was
  20| replaced with seasonal worker s. Much of the Tow n wide landscape maintena nce is outsourced,
  21| resulting in a significant savings.   The multi-use track complex re mains under the control of the
  22| Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  23| CEMETERY
  24| One Cemetery Division Superintendent , a DPW employee, handles the dut ies of the
  25| North and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting
  26| regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment
  27| and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  28| As always,  I am deeply appreciative  of the continued patience and support of our
  29| residents and fellow Town employees as we persevere through these difficult times.
  30| “As caretakers of the Town‟s infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the
  31| resources of the T own of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or
  32| exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella
  33| of public works”

   2| During FY 2014 and FY 2015 we continued our efforts in several areas: technology, collection
   3| development and programs.  We completed an extensive overhaul of our technical infrastructure which
   4| included creating an entirely new website (www.lunenburglibrary.org).  We have had great feedback on
   5| the website and are adding new aspects and information to it all the time. We included a link so the public
   6| can easily give us feedback or ask a question of the Library staff. The LPL also “virtualized” all of our
   7| public computers.  This has been a huge success in both gaining efficiency – we decreased the time it
   8| takes to open the Library by 50%.  By using NComputing virtualization devices we decreased our energy
   9| consumption by 70% and also decreased our computer maintenance cost by about 600% as we do not
  10| need to purchase, maintain, or replace CPUs any longer for our public computers.  We also added a new
  11| Wi-Fi access point to increase signal strength and access.  The Library was also part of a State-wide
  12| installation of fiber optic cable for improved access to the internet for rural communities. We partnered
  13| with the Town‟s Green Committee Task Force to migrate our exterior lighting to LED for more energy
  14| efficiency. In FY2016 we hope to do the same with our interior lighting.
  15| In terms of services and programs we have continued our weekly PC Tech Sessions with Doug Pulsifer
  16| and also added a new bi-weekly E-Book Tech Session so you can learn how to operate and download all
  17| kinds of E-content.  The LPL has begun to reach out to other community organizations and partner with
  18| them to bring services and programs to Lunenburg through the library.  One example of this was
  19| providing one-on-one help with navigating new health insurance choices.   We also help to promote the
  20| sale of the Friendly Seniors May Baskets which help support their organization and we hosted the Girl
  21| Scouts car-wash and many scout meetings. The Eagle Scouts worked on a face lift of our beautiful garden
  22| at the back of the Library.  The Friends hosted their Cultural Horizon series of events, this time focusing
  23| on the Scandinavian culture.  Sandy Altucher, our Digital Services Librarian has begun an ongoing
  24| weekly Writers Group – the one class/group very quickly became two due to the number of attendees.
  25| During the winter that WAS the Library had some very challenging times with broken pipes, damaged
  26| ceilings and rooms.  Our huge as in HUGE thanks to the DPW and the Fire Department for their quick
  27| action on that most horrible day for their quick response which saved us immeasurable damage to our
  28| collections and the building. We just, as May 21st, completed the repairs to the Library.  FY16 should
  29| bring us a new burner and building management system which will help us avoid similar disasters in the
  30| future.
  31| We said goodbye to our extraordinary Library Page, Jeff Dike, who went off to Boston and the
  32| Wentworth Institute of Technology.  New Library Pages, Shelby Scorse and Joe Rager were hired and
  33| quickly learned the ropes at the LPL.  One of the biggest, perhaps earth-shattering changes was the
  34| retirement of Kate McCarron, our Teen/YA Librarian for 23 years!!  Kate‟s creative and heart-filled
  35| approached to job will be sorely missed.

===PAGE 59=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 59
   2| SEWER COMMISSION
   3| The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006.
   4| At the May 2013 Town Election, Carl Luck and Mike Nault were re-elected for three year terms and the officers
   5| voted in were: Carl Luck –continuing as Chairman, Jay Simeone- Vice-Chairman and Dave MacDonald - Clerk.
   6| The other members are Butch Bilotta and Mike Nault. The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of
   7| overseeing the Town‟s sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and implementing rules,
   8| regulations and policies.  The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side of the
   9| system.  Under the Town Charter responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the
  10| sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager.  The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a
  11| sewer treatment plant. Approximately 76% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the
  12| other 24% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  13| There are currently 730 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 390 that have access to town
  14| sewer but are not yet connected. During Fiscal Year 2014, forty new accounts connected to town sewer.  The
  15| Commission billed out $656,440 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $53,730 of prior year delinquencies added to
  16| the owners‟ property tax bills for non-payment.   Sixteen properties with access to sewer were assessed privilege
  17| fees that had not originally been assessed betterments.  Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the
  18| Town on the original sewer project.
  19| The Sewer Commission was very proud to present a balanced budget during the FY15 budget process during
  20| FY14. This means that for the second time it was not necessary to transfer any monies from Retained Earnings
  21| to operate the Sewer Division of the DPW, which had been the case every year prior to FY14.The goal of the
  22| Sewer Commission is to maintain a balanced budget going forward and to build up the Retained Earnings so that
  23| it may used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system.
  24| In the spring of 2013, the Sewer Commission solicited proposals to convert from in-house service of the 10
  25| pumping stations and sewer infrastructure to having an outside contractor perform the service and maintenance.
  26| The Sewer Commission selected Small Water Systems Services of Littleton for a one-year contract with the
  27| potential for up to three years starting in August 2013. Corey Dowd who had served as the Sewer Technician
  28| moved over to the Highway Division of the DPW.   As the fiscal year 2014 drew to a close, the Commission has
  29| been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with Small Water Systems Services and has
  30| already put into motion to add to their responsibilities the maintenance of the 10 generators housed in our
  31| pumping stations with the next contract year. The Commission also worked hard over the course of FY14 with
  32| Small Water to explore the costs and benefits of installing SCADA systems in the pumping stations which
  33| allows for remote checking of alarms, hopefully reducing the number of call outs and expense.
  34| The Sewer Commission had several large projects throughout FY14.  In September 2013, the revision and
  35| implementation of the Sewer Use Regulations became effective. With this document, all bylaws, policies, rules
  36| and regulations were compiled into one document. One of the new policies developed and utilized this year was
  37| the Illegal Connection Policy for use when a property connects to the town sewer without obtaining the proper
  38| permits.  A minimum fee of $5000 will be levied to the property owner along with back usage charges if the
  39| Commission learns of an illegal connection.  The Commission offered “amnesty” to anyone who knowingly was
  40| connected and was not paying a sewer bill from the inception of the policy through October 2013.  In
  41| Lunenburg, the water and sewer is billed separately, so a resident would know that they are illegally connected
  42| if they do not have a septic system and do not receive a quarterly sewer bill. The Commission would encourage
  43| any resident in that situation come forward to rectify it versus having the illegal connection discovered and be
  44| subject to substantial fines.  The Commission also worked to enforce the Grease Trap Regulation for all food
  45| service establishments that have or should have a grease trap or grease interceptor.   Annual permit fees were
  46| assessed in October 2013 based on the establishment‟s level of compliance and the reporting requirements
  47| clearly communicated.  Lunenburg has 19 grease trap establishments.  This regulation will continue to be
  48| strongly enforced as fats, oils and grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  49| costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal,

===PAGE 60=== (plain, 52 lines)
   1| 60
   2| individual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases
   3| everyone‟s costs. The Commission understands that education is an important aspect of their role and will
   4| continue to provide information to their customers to keep the sewer system running economically and
   5| efficiently by reducing the disposal of fats, oil and grease into the system to avoid blockages. Whether on sewer
   6| or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.  Another enemy of the sewer system is “disposable wipes”
   7| which do not bio-degrade and bring our pumps to a halt.  Nothing but organic waste and toilet paper should be
   8| put into the sewer system. In July 2013 when the State required that Leominster implement a “sewer bank fee”
   9| for the mandated Inflow/Infiltration remediation, they passed along that request to those towns which have flow
  10| processed by Leominster. The Lunenburg Sewer Commission adopted the I/I Sewer bank fee effective 7/30/13,
  11| to be collected along with the standard connection fee.  The Commission, working with Weston & Sampson
  12| Engineering, continued their work to find sources of I/I (Inflow/Infiltration) by performing a house-to-house
  13| inspection in the Whalom area in the Spring of 2014, exactly what the Sewer Bank is designed for; to ensure
  14| that the Commonwealth of Massachusetts towns work to eliminate extraneous sources flowing into sewer
  15| treatment systems.
  16| A major goal of the Commission is to increase sewer revenues by adding new customers both within the
  17| currently sewered areas as well as identified needs areas per the 2010 Town-funded Comprehensive Wastewater
  18| Management Plan (CWMP). During FY14, the Commission worked closely with several resident groups
  19| interested in sewer extensions to connect to public sewer.  A group of seven residents on Lancaster Ave. worked
  20| successfully with the Commission on the installation of a privately-funded sewer extension from Whiting St.
  21| and accepted by the Town on November 7, 2013.  A second group came forward with a request for the
  22| Commission‟s assistance to extend the sewer from Prospect Avenue to the Pine Grove Road area, as well as the
  23| Sunset Avenue area.  This last extension would serve 42 parcels and eliminate an environmental concern for
  24| Whalom Lake. A second group came forward with a request for the Commission‟s assistance to extend the
  25| sewer from Prospect Ave. to the Pine Grove Rd. area, as well as the Sunset Ave. area.  This last extension would
  26| serve 42 parcels and eliminate an environmental concern for Whalom Lake.  Another area of town that had
  27| come forward in the past with interest in connecting to sewer (Pratt St. & Rennie St.) was included in the same
  28| application to the State.  Both areas had two thirds support of the residents, so the Commission contracted with
  29| Wright Pierce, who had worked with the town on the 2010 CWMP, to put together the SRF Proposal application
  30| (PEF). It was submitted to the State to apply for low-interest loans from the State Revolving Fund (SRF) in
  31| August 2013.   The proposal attained a sufficient rating to be assured of an SRF loan and the Commission
  32| presented the project at the spring Town Meeting in May 2014 and obtained approval to move the project
  33| forward.  These extensions as proposed would have little impact to the system costs but would improve the
  34| bottom line by providing more revenue to the Sewer Enterprise Fund.  Also during FY14 an additional private
  35| extension was brought before the Commission to connect a residence on Highland Avenue to the sewer main on
  36| Oak Avenue.
  37| The Commission continued to work diligently with the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen and the City of Fitchburg
  38| to complete an updated Inter-Municipal Agreement (IMA), which went into effect 12/15/13, include converting
  39| the basis by which we pay Fitchburg to the amount of outflow as measured by in-ground flow meters at the town
  40| line.   A major accomplishment of the new IMA was the elimination of a use “adder” which was a significant
  41| portion of the cost. These two issues have reduced the cost of sending flow to Fitchburg, as the switch to the
  42| meter basis went into effect in February 2014.  The new IMA also includes terms for Lunenburg to pay for its
  43| portion of Fitchburg‟s proposed John Fitch Highway improvement project in order to secure a guarantee of
  44| access for future projected needs. This represents the first time Lunenburg has had to pay directly for capacity
  45| and during the remainder of FY14 the Commission worked to create the new Reserve Capacity Fee policy,
  46| which went into effect at the end of FY14.  This policy ensures a fair sharing of the cost of purchasing additional
  47| capacity to new users.  Chairman Carl Luck and past Chairman and current Board of Selectmen liaison, Bob
  48| Ebersole worked tirelessly with Fitchburg officials to get this agreement renewed with terms that are more
  49| favorable to the Town of Lunenburg.  The Sewer Commission is grateful to them for their hard work and
  50| perseverance in getting this important agreement between Fitchburg and Lunenburg finalized.   The
  51| Commission also wishes to thank Joe Jordan, who stepped down as the director of the Fitchburg Wastewater
  52| Division in June 2014 for his hard work and wishes him the best in his new endeavor.

   2| TECHNOLOGY
   3| The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through
   4| the use of technology.  The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to
   5| communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and share information, and
   6| generally conduct business more effectively.  We also provide the necessary infrastructure,
   7| equipment, software, and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when
   8| interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   9| Our staff has remained the same this year, but our network administrator Josh Branham has taken
  10| on a full time position following graduating from Fitchburg State.
  11| Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director
  12|  Hired in December, 2012
  13|  Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA
  14| and Belchertown Public Schools
  15| Josh Branham – Network Administrator
  16|  Over six years working for the town and school system
  17|  Stepping into a full time role this year having successfully completed his Bachelor‟s
  18| Degree in Computer Information Systems – Congratulations Josh!
  19| Alex Smith – Systems Administration (part time)
  20|  Over three years working for the town and school system
  21|  Currently half way through his senior year at Fitchburg State majoring in Computer
  22| Science
  23| Along with serving as helpdesk for all users‟ daily technology issues and the usual maintenance
  24| and scheduled replacement of the town‟s and schools‟ technology assets, the IT Department has
  25| been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year.  The following
  26| examples are among the more noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  27| We have continued our efforts to virtualize all server hardware via VMware vSphere.  Lunenburg
  28| High School, Middle School, and Primary School, as well as the Town Hall and Public Safety
  29| complex servers have all been converted to virtual machines.  This conversion provides greatly
  30| enhanced management and backup, while creating a hardware-independent environment.  If a
  31| physical server fails, the virtual servers can be moved to almost any available hardware,
  32| simplifying disaster recovery.
  33| An enterprise-style Wi-Fi solution has been deployed at the Public Safety Complex.  Replacing
  34| older, consumer grade equipment has given us universal coverage within the complex.  The
  35| system also offers an open guest network enabling visitors to access the internet, while
  36| simultaneously providing a secure connection to internal networks and services for our police
  37| officers, fire fighters, EMS personnel and administration.
  38| The technology department also assisted with the implementation of fleet management software
  39| for the Department of Public Works.  Keeping track of use and maintenance schedules is
  40| absolutely critical for an organization which relies on a large and varied fleet of vehicles to

   1| 63
   2| TECHNOLOGY
   3| perform the ongoing tasks of keeping Lunenburg‟s physical infrastructure safe and functional for
   4| all town residents and visitors.

   6| am very happy to have stepped into this role, and I couldn‟t be more pleased with the level of
   7| skill, hard work and dedication of my staff, Josh and Alex.  I know that because of them we will

   9| reputation they helped to garner over the past several years.

   1| 74
   2| FIRE DEPARTMENT
   3| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   4| dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
   5| the citizens of the Town of Lunenburg.
   6| In 2014 the Fire Department responded to 1957 calls for service as follows:
   7| Fires & Hazardous Conditions                 521                Emergency Medical      914
   8| Inspections                  458                  Other                             64
   9| Fire Department staffing today is five career staff and forty- forty-five on call firefighters and
  10| emergency medical technicians.  The station is staffed Monday through Friday from 6 a.m. - 6 p.m.
  11| However, on weeknights and weekends all of our personnel respond from home on an “on call” basis.
  12| This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the station, pick up
  13| the appropriate equipment and then respond to the call.  This staffing model has not seen a substantial
  14| change in over 30 years, even though we have experienced a nearly 400% increase in calls for service
  15| during that period.  While we have added four additional full time personnel in that time to bolster our
  16| day time response, it has only resulted in a 12% increase in the time the station is manned.  We still rely
  17| on our on call personnel to be the primary responders 64% of the week.
  18| 2014 was our busiest year to date, and given the growth occurring in town , I can only anticipate
  19| further increases in service requests.   Whi le the commitment shown by our call f orce is nothing short of
  20| amazing, the ever increasing call volume is straining our ability to pro vide the high level of service
  21| townspeople have come to expect.  These factors lead me to continue to ca ll for 24 hour staffing in the
  22| station.  Sufficient funding to maintain two Firefighter/EMT‟ s in the  station 24 hours a day, 7 days a
  23| week would allow either the first ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond immediately.  Not
  24| only would this reduce our response times to critical emergencies, it would also red uce the “wear and
  25| tear” on our call staff.   This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our on call personnel; it would
  26| only supplement their response and reduce response times.
  27| Our firefighters continue to train for the myriad of situations which can be encountered even in a
  28| town such as ours.  Today‟s firefighte r must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care,
  29| hazardous materials mitigation and technical rescue.  Our EMT‟s must continue to stay abreast of the
  30| latest treatment protocols and advances in lifesaving skills.  Personnel tr ain regularly both here at the
  31| station and at classes sponsored by other agencies.  While they are compensated for some of the training,
  32| the vast majority is done on their own time.  To give an example, a new Firefighter/EMT must attend over
  33| 340 hours of basic firefighting and emergency medical t echnician training.  Once they are certif ied they
  34| must complete over 40 hours of continuing education and refresher training every two years, jus t for their
  35| EMT.  Ope rators of fire a pparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of training in pump and aerial
  36| operations, hydraulics calculations and vehicle operation.
  37| Several of our personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing servic es for
  38| Confined Space and Cave i n Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery.  They stand ready to
  39| respond as needed throughout Northern Worcester County.
  40| 2014 continued to be an active year for our fire prevention efforts.   By law we are required to
  41| inspect smoke and carbon monoxide d Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to not only
  42| children in our schools but adults as well.    Other fire safety initiatives undertaken during 2014 include:

===PAGE 75=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 75
   2| - Implementation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $4,900 to assist with
   3| fire safety e ducation in both the schools and with senior c itizens.  This program, known as
   4| SAFE and Senior SAFE was renewed in late 2014 with an additional $7,200 in grant funding.
   5| - Participation in the senior c enter‟s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provides
   6| buckets of walkway sand for senior c itizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in
   7| the winter months.  In partnership with this program, our Fire Prevention Officer Captain
   8| James Ricci would deliver the sand to the residents and when possible, inspect thei r homes
   9| for smoke and carbon monoxide d etectors.  If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci
  10| would install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.   Capt. Ri cci has
  11| installed well over 50 smoke and carbon monoxide d etectors for our s eniors since the
  12| inception of this program.
  13| - Lieutenant Mark Bursch completed the Fire Academy‟s “Public Life Safety Educator”
  14| Program.  This provides us another certified educator to provide fire and life safety
  15| educational programs to the public.
  16| Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both
  17| citizens and town employees.
  18| Our d epartment continues to actively pursue grant f unding opportunities as they occur, though
  19| those opportunities are becoming scarcer.
  20| The new ambulance funded under the FY ‟14 Capital Plan was received in late June and placed in
  21| service as Rescue 1.  The unit has seen steady service since then and will service the community for many
  22| years to come.  The former Rescue 1 (2008 vintage) was re-designated Rescue 2 and serves as our back -
  23| up ambulance.  The 2004 vintage unit was traded in.
  24| The replacement for Engine 1 approved in the FY ‟15 Capital Plan has been ordered and is
  25| expected to arrive in May 2015.  This new unit will become our “first due” engine and will replace a 1991
  26| model.
  27| Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern.  FY‟15 has seen a significant increase in
  28| vehicle maintenance costs, particularly with the older apparatus.  The replacements of the above units will
  29| hopefully decrease some of our vehicle maintenance costs.  However, two of our front line units (Engine
  30| 4 and Ladder 1) are over 20 years old and showing their age through increasing maintenance costs and
  31| “out of service” time.   These units are included the d epartment‟s 5 Year Capital Plan for replacement in
  32| FY ‟17 and FY ‟18 respectively and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely
  33| replacement.  The high reliability required of front line emergency apparatus becomes increasingly
  34| expensive and difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
  35| Firefighters Matthew Benoit, Michael Byrne and Timothy Downey  graduated from the
  36| Massachusetts Firefighting Academy‟s Call/Volunteer Training Program.  This intensive 6 month
  37| program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today‟s challenging environments.   All
  38| newly hired f irefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire.  In addition,
  39| Firefighters Benoit, Downey, P. Andrew Duquette and J ames Reynolds obtained their certification as
  40| Emergency Medical Technicians.
  41| In October 2014 two Lunenburg Firefighters were recognized at the Annual Firefighter of the
  42| Year Awards, held at the JFK Library in Boston.  This was the first time that any of o ur firefighters have
  43| received this recognition in the 25 year history of the a wards.  Captain James Ricci received the Norman
  44| Knight Award for his outstanding efforts in Fire Prevention and Safety throughout the Community.
  45| Firefighter Austin Flagg receive d recognition for his rescue of a badly injured resident from a structure
  46| fire in January.

===PAGE 77=== (plain, 18 lines)
   1| 77
   2| POLICE DEPARTMENT
   3| CHIEF JAMES P. MARINO
   4| “Police must secure the willing co-operation of the public in voluntary observance of the law to be able to secure and
   5|   maintain the respect of the public.”
   6|   Sir Robert Peel
   7| Mission Statement
   8| The mission of the Lunenburg Police Department is defined by a more advanced style of policing that emphasizes personal
   9| service and community problem resolution, and accentuates the uncompromising integrity, trust, respect, and accountability, of
  10| the police officers who are committed to providing a safe and secure environment for every citizen.
  11| The men and women of the Lunenburg Police Department are required to accept, support, and carry out this mission in a
  12| manner consistent with our core values and with distinction, given all practical resources, in the interest of the protection
  13| of life and property, preventing crime, and preserving peace.
  14| Core Values
  15| Courage—we, the men and women of the Lunenburg Police Department, accept as our primary obligation to be
  16| physically, morally, and mentally strong against the challenges we bear as law enforcement officers; always standing firm
  17| in the face of danger, confronting threats without apprehension, placing the safety and security of others before our own
  18| with honor.

===PAGE 78=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 78
   2| Integrity—we possess an uncompromising sense of rectitude with undying respect and concern for humanity while
   3| safeguarding the dignity of all people; we are committed to the highest level of moral principles and ethics in our actions
   4| and appearance worthy of the public trust; never placing ourselves above the law and always inspiring the confidence of
   5| our community whether on or off duty.
   6| Commitment—we will work together as a team, promoting leadership within the organization, among our peers and
   7| throughout the community; we are committed to faithfully meeting the demands of our profession, achieving
   8| organizational goals that serve the public, and performing  all of our duties to the best of our abilities with dedication and
   9| determination; preserving the reputation and morale of the police department; always accepting responsibility for our
  10| actions, always consistent, fair and impartial; treating all citizens with dignity and respect, and always concerned with the
  11| welfare and quality of life for all people.

  41| Reportable incidents and Field Interviews

  48| Incidents, Arrests, Calls For Service, & Crime Rates
  49| Calls for service have increased by 13.3 percent over last year, nearly twice the amount predicted last year, and 35.7%
  50| over the past five years. More than 3000 man hours were used this year responding to calls.
  51| Reportable incidents and Field Interviews are all crimes that did not originate as arrests, and would need to be investigated
  52| and reported on or an interview with a suspect or victim was necessary and reported grew by 8.3% over last year.
  53| Incidents and Field Interviews have risen over the past five years by 80.8% with an average annual rate of 12%.
  54| Officers expend an enormous amount of time investigating, following up, and reporting on incidents. This year the man
  55| hours to complete just reports for incidents and field interviews exceeded 1700.
  56| Arrests, Protective Custody and Criminal Complaint Summons have increased by 47.3% over last year and 78.6% over
  57| the past five years with an average annual rate of 15%.   To assure the safety of the officers and detainees department
  58| policy requires a minimum of two officers for all arrests and bookings. The average arrests including, processing at the
  59| scene of arrest, transporting to the police lock-up, booking, processing evidence, completing arrest reports and related
  60| documents, and then transporting the prisoner to court takes approximately four (4) hours.
  61| Many time two officers will be scheduled per shift. When an arrest commits both officers additional help will be called in
  62| at the overtime rate to handle calls.
  63| Violent crime rose to 6% above last year with Simple assaults responsible most of the increase. Property crimes increased
  64| by 28%. Burglaries went down with Shoplifting/Larceny, Forgery and Fraud driving the increase. Drug and weapons
  65| violations cause crimes against society to increase 63%. Disorderly conduct, alcohol related crime, driving under the
  66| influence and trespassing topped the Group B crimes with a 64% increase.
  67| The rise in crime can be attributed to many factors such as the unemployment rate. The increase in drug related crimes
  68| could be due to a sharp rise in opioid addiction. Modes of transportation have increased. The geographical location places

===PAGE 81=== (plain, 37 lines)
   1| 81
   2| Group B Crimes
   3|  Bad Checks
   4|  Disorderly Conduct
   5|  Driving Under the Influence
   6|  Drunkenness
   7|  Liquor Law Violations
   8|  Runaways (18 or under)
   9|  Trespass of Real Property
  10|  All Other offenses
  11| (The largest increases were Trespass and Alcohol
  12| Related Crime)
  13| Increase of 64 % over last year
  14| Lunenburg on the border of the two largest cities in Worcester County. Criminal Offenders from other jurisdictions
  15| account for a significant part of the crime rate.  Other factors include increase in population, effective strength of law
  16| enforcement, administrative and investigative emphasis of law enforcement; areas that could be improved with increased
  17| staffing levels.
  18| Current Issues
  19| As of December 20, 2015 the 3 pm to 7 pm, Monday thru Friday, Desk Watch officer position has been discontinued.
  20| Lack of funding will not allow me to keep the public safety building open during those hours.
  21| As I have continually emphasized; leaving a public safety building un-manned in a community such as ours raises
  22| fundamental questions, irrespective of fiscal restraint, about  the police department‟s and fire department‟s day to day
  23| operations, the services demanded by the public, and  the safety and security of the building and the community.
  24| The importance of the desk officer position with respect to the service it represents is evident. A sample of data revealed
  25| that that in a 35 day period, from the 2nd of September to October 21st, 35 days, 709 calls were logged and the public
  26| visited the lobby for police or fire service 414 times.  The day shift desk officer investigated 136 complaints in the lobby
  27| over the past year; larcenies, missing persons, violations of restraining orders, served harassment orders, restraining
  28| orders, motor vehicle accidents, motor vehicle complaints, neighbor disputes, medical emergencies, child abuse annoying
  29| Group A Crimes Against Persons
  30|  Forcible Rape
  31|  Sexual Assault
  32|  Aggravated Assault
  33|  Simple Assault     (Responsible for the largest
  34| increase)
  35|  Intimidation
  36|  Statutory Rape
  37| Increase of 6% above Last Year

===PAGE 82=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 82
   2| phone calls, threats, trespassing, vandalism, sexual assault, processed 61 firearms licenses, registered 6 sex offenders just
   3| to name a few. The 3 pm to 7 pm shift which was cut back in December, logged nearly half again that of the daytime desk
   4| officer.
   5| Staffing issues never seem to go away. The town has far outgrown its police department. The increased demand for
   6| service with inadequate staffing has a direct effect on our ability to decrease overtime, reduces officer initiated activities,
   7| and compromises our crime clearance rate. The demand for service has increased 35.7% over the last five years. Calls for
   8| service, arrests, criminal complaints, and incidents continue to increase dramatically. Patrolling the streets to increase
   9| visibility, to reduce the fear of crime, or prevent crime has become problematic again this year because the work load has
  10| increased. Again this year officers had to be called in or were held over their regular scheduled shifts to meet the work
  11| load demand.  In the first six months of Fiscal 2015 officers were called in for various reasons 104 times, a 44.4%
  12| increase over last year. As I have said in the past, growth will continue to bridge the gap between staffing levels and the
  13| workload.  Services will never rise to an acceptable level without a meaningful increase in the amount of police officers
  14| we employ.
  15| Emergency response will never change. The police will always have to respond to emergencies, and the community will
  16| have to provide an adequate level of police staffing to perform this function with respect to reasonable response times,
  17| with respect to the safety of the public and with the safety of police officers in mind, and not the least of which to reduce
  18| exposure to civil liability.
  19| Non-emergency response where officers respond to calls and collect information and evidence and take statements
  20| necessary to solve crimes, will also continue to climb in numbers with social change and growth.
  21| Quality of life issues come under the classification of officer initiated calls. An example of this level of policing is traffic
  22| enforcement and crime prevention. The public expects the same service with both these levels of law enforcement and
  23| these are the services that suffer the most from budget cuts and inadequate staffing. When the police have no time to
  24| properly investigate crimes, engage the community to solve problems, or provide quick emergency response because of
  25| the increased demands placed on individual officers due to poor staffing levels, then the community will quickly see the
  26| police as ineffective. The community will be less and less interested in supporting a service that has no value.
  27| Thank you again this year for allowing me to serve as your Chief of Police. I fully appreciate and I value, and I thank you
  28| for the support the town has shown the police department.  I humbly ask for your uncompromising commitment going
  29| forward as I seek funding for additional staffing and desk service at the public safety building so that I can provide the
  30| services that Lunenburg needs, that Lunenburg deserves, and that Lunenburg is entitled to.
  31| Despite all these challenges we face, Lunenburg is still a safe place to reside or visit. I can assure you the men and women
  32| of the Lunenburg Police Department stand by our promise to carry out our mission and to realize our vision; to give the
  33| best service, to guarantee the greatest security and provide safest environment for every citizen.
  34| Respectfully submitted,
  35| Chief James P Marino
  36| Chief James P. Marino

   2| CAPITAL PLANNING
   3| Lunenburg‟s Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal
   4| Year (FY) Plan and a Capital Improvement Plan. These plans are to be presented to the Town
   5| Manager.
   6| As noted in the past reports, the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety,
   7| Need, Crisis, Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Capital Planning Committee has to
   8| review each request under these six elements. Not only do they have to determine that the
   9| request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria.
  10| Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the
  11| proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and protection of the public, the
  12| upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  13| At the Annual Town Meeting May 2014, the following items, totaling $ 1,114,152, were
  14| presented in the FY15 Plan.

   1| 88
   2| ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   3| The Zoning Board of Appeals heard many petitions during calendar year 2014.   There were several
   4| applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board
   5| meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an “as needed” basis.
   6| The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Donald F. Bowen, Chairman, Raymond Beal,
   7| Vice Chairman, James Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle, Hans Wentrup and Associate memb ers, David Blatt,
   8| Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi.   Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning
   9| Board.
  10| The functions of the Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  11| 1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town‟s Protective By-Laws as authorized in
  12| the By-laws.
  13| 2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town‟s Protective By-
  14| laws.
  15| 3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts
  16| General Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain
  17| a permit or enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Boar d in the Town, the
  18| Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  19| 4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General
  20| Laws, Chapter 40B.  The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews request s for comprehensive permits
  21| submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.
  22| These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  23| The Office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  24| Avenue.  If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146
  25| X 421 during regular business hours.  Zoning Board applications are available in the office of the Town
  26| Clerk and online at www.Lunenburgonline.com.

   1| 92
   2| Superintendent’s Message
   3| I am honored to report to you another on anot her exciting and productive year for the Lunenburg
   4| Public Schools.  Certainly the start of construction on the new middle -high school is a highlight.
   5| It has been inspiring to see the changes to the campus landscape and watch the building and one
   6| of three new fields be constructed.  Site enabling began summer 2014 as local contractor Powell
   7| Stone and Gravel started removing topsoil and trees so construction could get underway.  We all
   8| experienced a sense of loss with the removal of most of the trees from th e front lawn, but that
   9| feeling was soon replaced with excitement as the footprint of the new building was made distinct
  10| and the grading, leveling, irrigation and sod installation on the front field progressed.  The
  11| ceremonial groundbreaking was held Octobe r 18, 2014 in conjunction with Homecoming.  What
  12| a proud and glorious day that was for our community!  Certainly the best speeches of that day
  13| were delivered by students Lauren Basque , President of the Class of 2016, the last class to
  14| graduate from the currently Lunenburg High School and Brendan Tobin, President of the Class of
  15| 2017, the first class to graduate from the new school.  Planning for the next milestone ceremony,
  16| the “Topping Off” is underway. This ceremony is a steelworker‟s tradition for placin g the last
  17| beam.
  18| The building project progressed from site enabling to the pouring of footings and frost walls.
  19| Work on the front field was completed in mid -October.  The sod and grass require at least one
  20| growing season before use and the intent is to bring the field on line no later than  Fall 2015.
  21| School athletes and Town youth program participants and their coaches have cooperated well to
  22| share the limited field space that remains during construction with all soccer programs needing to
  23| go off-site.  I want to recognize and thank Athletic Director Peter McCauliff and members of the
  24| Lunenburg youth sports groups for their efforts in scheduling the limited fields.
  25| Structural steel arrived at the site in December and the first column was placed Decemb er 8,
  26| 2014.  It was remarkable how quickly the erection of the steel progressed, and by January the
  27| high school wing and auditorium steel were in place and the high school wing wrapped.   The
  28| temporary tent enclosure with temporary heating and lighting services allows work to continue in
  29| winter conditions.  The historic snow fall and frigid temperature in the final days of January and
  30| through February slowed, but did not halt progress.  In the final week of February, as I write this
  31| report, construction act ivities include: steel erection, roof and second floor decking installation,
  32| temporary utility service work and underground mechanical/electrical/plumbing (MEP) work.

   2| School Facilities
   3| 2014-2015
   4| Site work began on the Lunenburg Middle/High School project in July 2014. Shawmut
   5| Construction Management is executing the project with oversight by Joslin, Lesser & Associates
   6| and the School Building Committee. Foundation work began in September and was completed in
   7| early December. The building‟s steel structure began to be installed in December 2014 and is
   8| expected to be complete by the end of March 2015
   9| The FY15 capital plan approved upgrades to the front end controllers of the Building
  10| Management Systems at Lunenburg Primary School at a total cost of $14,000. This work was
  11| awarded to Control Technologies and was completed by September 2014. Also funded in the
  12| FY15 Capital Plan was $138,000 to abate asbestos at Turkey Hill Middle School. The asbestos
  13| abatement work was bid in May 2014 but came in at $175,000 so no work was completed during
  14| the summer of 2014. The scope of the work has been reduced and is expected to be rebid in April
  15| 2015.
  16| The focus of the school maintenance effort for FY15 continued to concentrate on preventative
  17| maintenance of heat and ventilation systems and energy conservation.
  18| The Town of Lunenburg successfully applied as a Green Community in 2013. Projects that were
  19| completed for Turkey Hill Middle School in the summer of 2014 include replacement of the
  20| building‟s climate control air compressor with a smaller unit and installation of variable speed
  21| drives on the building heating circulation pumps. Lunenburg Primary School energy efficiency
  22| measures planned included lighting upgrades, however they were not funded. Efforts during the
  23| year were directed at finding a more cost effective approach to upgrade the lighting systems.
  24| For the FY16 Capital Program, the School Department requested an additional $34,500 for
  25| asbestos abatement for Turkey Hill Middle School on a multi-year abatement plan.  Also for the
  26| Middle School was a request to upgrade the phone system and the intercom system at $22,000
  27| each. Requests were also submitted for the District Technology plan at $36,200, a replacement
  28| grounds shed for $32,000 and $38,656 to begin to prepare to move the district headquarters to the
  29| Turkey Hill Middle School during the summer of 2016. As of the time of this report, it would
  30| appear that no funds will be appropriated for any of the FY16 requests.
  31| Respectfully submitted,
  32| John Londa, Director of Facilities
