   2| Special Services Department
   3| Town Report – FY14 (data taken from 2013-2014 school year)
   4| Julianna Hanscom, Director of Special Services
   5| There are two hundred and seventy -eight students with disabilities receiving special
   6| education or related services in Lunenburg.  This represents 17.8% of our total student population
   7| of 1,564 students (it was 18.4% last year).  This percentage is slightly more than the state average
   8| of 17%.  As of March 1, 2014, the total number of students receiving services outsi de the district
   9| was twenty -six.  Of the twenty -six students, seven are serviced in Collaborative Placements,
  10| thirteen are in Day Placements and six are in Residential Placements.  During the 2013 -2014
  11| school year (8/28/13 – 6/19/14) seventy -one students id entified with a disability received
  12| accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
  13| This year as a district, our Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with both
  14| Turkey Hill Middle and Lunenburg High Schools listed as Level 2.  The state report card l ists
  15| Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as “Did Not Meet Target”.  The district‟s Students with
  16| Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps [Cumulative Progress and Performance
  17| Index (CPI): 1-100)] is listed at 54 (it was 50 last year) wi th the target set for 75.  Students with
  18| Disabilities participated in MCAS this year with the following rates:  English Language Arts =
  19| 97%, Mathematics = 98%, and Science = 96%.  This data can be found at the DESE
  20| websitehttp://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=0162
  21| 0000&orgtypecode=5&
  22| Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the
  23| whole Special Services Department.  DESE requires us to collect data for certain Indicators they
  24| are targeting each year.  For our Indicators, the following information is available below and also
  25| can be found at:
  26| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&
  27| Indicator 1 – Graduation Rate. For the 2012 -2013 school year, the state target for Students with
  28| Disabilities was 80.0%. Lunenburg surpassed the target with 82.4% and the overall state rate was
  29| only 67.8% for Students with Disabilities.
  30| Indicator 2 – Dropout Rate.  For the 2012 -2013 school year, the state target for Students with
  31| Disabilities was 4.3%. Lunenburg surpasse d the target with 0% and the overall state rate was
  32| 2.9% for Students with Disabilities.
  33| Indicator 3C  – Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS.  For the 2013 MCAS,
  34| Lunenburg Students with Disabilities CPI ratings were as follows:

   7| Indicator 4 – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For Lunenburg,
   8| our rate was only .3%.  The state rate was 1.1%.   Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant
   9| discrepancies with the Suspension/Expulsion rates by race or ethnicity.  In 2013, Lunenburg was
  10| not found to have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.
  11| Indicator 5 -  Educational Environments for Students Aged 6 -21 with IEPs. For 2012 -2013, the
  12| state‟s target rates are as follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 59.7%, Partial Inclusion = “not
  13| identified”, Substantially Separate Placements = 14.5%, and for Separate Schools/Residential
  14| Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 5.5%.  In Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows :  Full
  15| Inclusion = 76.2%, Partial = 10.0%, Substantially Separate = 6.9% and Separate schools,
  16| Residential, etc. = 6.9%.
  17| Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3 -5 with IEPs.  The 2012-2013 state
  18| rates showed the percent of students rec eiving a majority of their special education and related
  19| services in a Full Inclusion early childhood program was 38.9%, in a Partial Inclusion Program
  20| was 37.2%, a Substantially Separate Placement (special education class, separate school, or
  21| residential facility) was 15.1%, and students not attending an early childhood program but
  22| receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was 8.9%.  In Lunenburg, our early
  23| childhood Full Inclusion rate was 45.7%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 5.1%, Substantia lly
  24| Separate rate was .3%, and Related Services only 1.0%.
  25| Indicator 9 & 10 -  Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and
  26| in Special Education Categories.  In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no
  27| disproportionality found in either case.
  28| Indicator 11  – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   T his indicator measures the % of children with
  29| parental consent to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is
  30| reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting
  31| year.  Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was
  32| 96.8%.
  33| Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  This indicator measures the % of children referred by
  34| Part C, found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and
  35| implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is
  36| noted as “Suppressed”.  Data is suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found
  37| eligible for special education services is fewer than 10.  In Lunenburg, only 2 students were
  38| referred during 2012-2013.
  39| Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16
  40| and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually
  41| updated and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including
  42| courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet t hose postsecondary goals, and
  43| annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg was rated 100% in
  44| compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 99.2%.
  45| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the % of
  46| students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2011-12 school year and self-reported post-
  47| school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement
  48| is defined through three measurements:
  49|  Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.

===PAGE 98=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 98
   2|  Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year
   3| of leaving high school.
   4|  Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some othe r postsecondary education
   5| or training program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one
   6| year of leaving high school.
   7| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged
   8| in a post-secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2012-13 school year, state
   9| targets for the three measurements of Post -School Outcomes are Measurement A: 44%;
  10| Measurement B: 77%; and Measurement C: 84%.  Lunenburg rates are as follows:  Measurement
  11| A = 71.4%, Measurement B = 100% and Measurement C = 100%.

  16| each month, 11:00AM, at the Thomas C. Passios Building, room 13.  The executive b oard is
  17| made up of Chairperson Kelly Harvey and Treasurer Pami Terren.  Several events and
  18| presentations were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in Lunenburg this

===PAGE 99=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 99
   2| LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   3| 2014-2015
   4| The Lunenburg Primary School is a child -centered school devoted to providing quality
   5| early childhood education for all students.  We are a community of teachers, students, and parents
   6| working together to create a safe and respectful environmen t for children.  Current knowledge
   7| and research of child development guides the school‟s beliefs and practices and provides the
   8| foundation for learning for all students.
   9| The Lunenburg Primary School services Preschool to Third grade students.  The Octob er
  10| 1, 2013 student count reported to the state was 527 students.  Our Preschool program services
  11| twelve, three year old students and thirty-three four year old students. We have five Kindergarten
  12| classes servicing one hundred and ten students. The class si zes in our Kindergarten programs
  13| range from 21 -22 students.  First grade has one hundred and twenty -seven students with class
  14| sizes ranging from 25 -26. Second grade has one hundred and twenty -three students with class
  15| sizes of 24-25 and the third grade has  one hundred and thirty two students with class sizes of 26 -
  16| 27.  We are utilizing all available space in the building to provide a child centered enriching
  17| educational program for all our students.
  18| Our Professional Development this year focused on ALICE  training, the Teacher
  19| Evaluation process, the Common Core curriculum, and Curriculum Mapping updates. The
  20| ALICE training is a proactive safety protocol used in the event of an active shooter.  This is
  21| necessary to enhance our current safety procedures and  processes.  All of our classroom teachers
  22| continued to use Responsive Classroom strategies as part of our Academic/Social/Emotional
  23| Learning Curriculum which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and logical
  24| consequences and academic choice.
  25| The Primary School community continues to strive for academic excellence.  Common
  26| assessments are in place in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K -3 in the areas
  27| of reading, writing, and math.  These common assessments serve to monito r the effectiveness of
  28| teacher instruction and drives instructional programming for our students.  We are now able to
  29| track progress throughout the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve
  30| instruction.  Students are identified quickly an d additional resources and direct instruction are
  31| provided to address the areas of concern identified.  The Primary School is a Title One school and
  32| we continue to utilize a Response to Intervention (Title One/RTI) approach to address student
  33| needs.  Our t iered instructional system insures that all students have the instruction necessary to
  34| continue good progress with their learning. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area
  35| of Language Arts (Reading and Writing) and Math.  The  first tier involves the largest number of
  36| students, and is fitted for universal instruction in the classroom.  Teachers are skilled in
  37| differentiating instruction to meet a large number of student needs in the classroom.  The second
  38| tier combines more classroom teacher su pport and Title One Support for students. Title One/RTI
  39| services are based on student needs that are targeted, individual, and involve more intensive
  40| interventions.  The third tier is the smallest and involves special services with very intense
  41| interventions.  The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only individualize
  42| instruction to meet the needs of the students who are struggling with learning but those students
  43| who are doing well and need to strive to achieve higher levels of learni ng.  With this model
  44| learning is more personalized and students are taught to become independent learners and
  45| problem solvers.

===PAGE 100=== (plain, 47 lines)
   1| 100
   2| One hundred and four 3rd grade students participated in the spring 2014 MCAS
   3| Assessment at the Primary School in March and May last year.  The results were released and
   4| sent home to parents on September 30th.  For our 3rd graders this was their first experience taking
   5| the state testing and the second time the MCAS testing has been completed at the Primary School.
   6| We worked to make sure that students understand that they are capable and prepared to take this
   7| assessment each year. Students prepared inspirational posters and we held our first MCAS Prep
   8| Rally where the students sang the MCAS song. Teachers stress that the ongoing learning
   9| opportunities students have experienced over the last several years have prepared them to perform
  10| well on this assessment.
  11| In the area of English Language Arts there were 68 students or 65% who scored in the advanced
  12| and proficient range of functioning (14 advanced, 54 proficient). 30 students or 29% were in the
  13| needs improvement range (24 of which scored 230 or greater, 15 students were within three
  14| points of achieving a proficient score). 6% (6) were in the warning range.  These results are
  15| similar to the previous year‟s result.  This continues to be an area of focus for the Primary School.
  16| In the area of Math 80 students or 78% of our 3rd graders scored in the Advanced and Proficient
  17| range of functioning (39 students- 38%-in the advanced range of functioning and 41 students
  18| (40%) in the proficient range of functioning). 16% scored in the needs improvement range of
  19| functioning with 10 students scoring 230 or greater in the High –needs improvement range.  Six
  20| students or 6% were in the warning range on the Math Assessment.  Last year 85% of the
  21| students who took the Math MCAS scored in the Advanced/Proficient range of functioning.
  22| These results again are pretty similar to last years.  We continue to maintain a higher number of
  23| students in the advanced range of functioning compared to two years ago.
  24| Our high needs students (students with disabilities, student requiring Title I services, students
  25| requiring ELL instruction and low income students) continue to lag behind their peers both in
  26| ELA and Math. Using the old No Child Left Behind comparison, Composite Performance Index
  27| (CPI), which is used to measure the extent to which students are progressing toward proficiency
  28| (a CPI of 100 is the target) our non-high need students, achieved a Composite Performance Index
  29| (CPI) score of 94.1; our high need students received a CPI of 73.6 in ELA.  In the area of math
  30| our students‟ considered to have non-high needs (CPI) scored 97 and our high needs students
  31| scored 77.1.  We continue to address these student achievement gaps by using Tiered
  32| Instructional strategies and direct instruction, Individualized Educational Plans and Curriculum
  33| Accommodation plans.
  34| Student Growth Data is not available for students in the 3rd grade as this is the first time they have
  35| taken the MCAS assessment.
  36| If you would like to access graphs and additional data related to MCAS please go to the following
  37| web page:
  38| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/achievement_level.aspx?linkid=32&orgcode=01620010&orgty
  39| pecode=6&
  40| Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and
  41| supportive of each of our members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and
  42| recognize the importance of building a community where members feel a sense of belonging and
  43| responsibility to contribute.  Our students have a right to be in an environment which is safe,
  44| promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
  45| The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school issues, review the
  46| annual budget for the school and to develop the School Improvement Plan as well as make
  47| revisions to the school‟s handbook.

   2| Members of the Council for the 2014 –2015 school year were:

===PAGE 103=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 103
   2| TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL 2014-2015
   3| Turkey Hill Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that
   4| support students‟ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-
   5| motivated, creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open
   6| honest communication.   The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school
   7| embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This
   8| philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills
   9| to be successful in high school. Turkey Hill Middle School is in its third year of servicing students in
  10| grades four through seven.
  11| PERCENT OF STUDENTS SCORING IN THE ADVANCED/PROFICIENT RANGE
  12| Grade Subject THMS State
  13| 4 ELA 54% 54%
  14| 4 Math 55% 52%
  15| 5 ELA 72% 64%
  16| 5 Math 72% 61%
  17| 5 Sci/Tech 66% 53%
  18| 6 ELA 75% 68%
  19| 6 Math 79% 60%
  20| 7 ELA 83% 72%
  21| 7 Math 70% 50%
  22| In reviewing the results of the 2014 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, in all tested content areas at all grade levels,
  23| we consistently produced scores that are at or above the state average in percentage of students who fall in
  24| the advanced and proficient range, in some cases, 20% over the state average. Although we are proud of
  25| our students‟ performance, we continue to analyze data in our monthly results faculty meetings designed
  26| to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has identified areas of improvement based upon
  27| the data and implemented strategies to improve their instructional practices that will help students
  28| succeed.
  29| For the second straight year, Turkey Hill Middle School fourth grade teachers continue to utilize and
  30| acknowledge the benefits of an interdisciplinary teaming model.  This model permits teachers to work
  31| together in teams of two or three in order to provide focused instruction in the areas of reading and
  32| mathematics. Our specially designed teaming model allows students to remain with their homeroom
  33| teacher for the majority of the day, but designates a reading, writing, and mathematics teacher on each
  34| team. Teachers can develop expertise as content area specialists and the administration team provides
  35| specific professional development in each focus area for continuous improvement and growth. This form
  36| of teaming also allows us to create more common experiences at the grade level through unit planning
  37| and shared assessments.
  38| Additionally, teaming helps prepare students for an increased level of expectations in reading, math, and
  39| writing when entering the fourth grade, while continuing to be sensitive to the social and emotional needs
  40| of this population.

===PAGE 104=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 104
   2| We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each
   3| individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule allow
   4| teachers time to work with students at their own individual skill level.  The philosophy is to challenge
   5| students who are high academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified
   6| weaknesses. Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions or
   7| provide more individualized support.  We design this support model to allow students to feel more
   8| confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.  We will continue to refine our intervention block times
   9| to best meet the needs of our students.
  10| Turkey Hill Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes
  11| provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health
  12| and guidance. Our Second step developmental guidance program is now consistent in grades four through
  13| seven and units focus on healthy decision making, empathy and communication as well  as bullying
  14| prevention to meet the intricate social needs of our middle school students.
  15| Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, library media, and music classes that allows
  16| them to explore their strengths and talents. This year, fourth and fifth grade students have been
  17| participating in a word processing enrichment class to improve their keyboarding skills. Students also
  18| have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment
  19| block is in addition to the traditional special area classes students receive, and this block offers students
  20| additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and
  21| more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  22| We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed
  23| to promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. This year we have
  24| refined and refreshed our utilization of responsive classroom and Developmental Design programs to
  25| deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with teachers in small groups to teach social
  26| responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and promoting positive self-esteem.
  27| Our seventh grade students are participating in themes such as embracing and respecting diversity and
  28| grades four and five students were experiencing themes developed through our second step guidance
  29| curriculum. We also incorporated an academic choice time for students in grades four and five. This is a
  30| time developed for students to explore different interests or experiences or utilize time to complete
  31| assignments that required additional time.
  32| We also utilize best practices such as peer mediation and the Big Sibs program to foster and develop
  33| positive peer leadership skills and effective peer mentoring.  All of the social emotional programs have
  34| been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals,
  35| and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  36| Turkey Hill Middle school continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student
  37| success and promotes lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to
  38| our success is our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and
  39| community who support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through
  40| their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  41| Respectfully submitted,
  42| Timothy Santry
  43| Principal

===PAGE 106=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 106
   2| LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
   3| Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2014 -2015.  As time
   4| has gone on, there is much more of a comfort le vel and stability in our grade 8 -12 community.
   5| This year, we oriented 128 eighth grade students, three teachers, and four staff members to our
   6| building and school culture.  With the assistance of 50 student leaders, we successfully
   7| transitioned the eighth grade students into the LHS culture by having an eighth orientation on the
   8| first day of school .  We opened the year, as we do every year, with a guest speaker from the
   9| Worcester County District Attorney‟s Office to discuss responsible Internet use, Facebook, cyber-
  10| bullying and Internet safety with our incoming students and parents.
  11| NEASC ACCREDITATION
  12| Lunenburg High School is in the process of completing a Five Year Report for the Commission
  13| on Public Secondary Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges.  In 2010,
  14| the Commission recommended that the school receive continued accreditation in the Association.
  15| The Commission's decision was based upon review of an evaluation report prepared by a visiting
  16| committee in April 2010.  The Two Year Progress Report, submitted in January, 2013 received
  17| high marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.
  18| While the Commission lauded LHS in many areas, the Commission warned LHS in three areas
  19| and asked for a Special Progress Report to be comple ted.  Of major concern were issues with the
  20| facility and meeting 21 st century learning expectations.  The Commission stated that the physical
  21| plant significantly limits the school‟s ability to meet the educational needs of students. There is an
  22| expectation to develop both a short - and long -term plan to fund and address the significant
  23| facilities concerns including inf rastructure and wiring issues, roof and window issues,  limited
  24| handicap accessibility and inadequate space.  In regards to curriculum, the co mmission
  25| recommended the further development of a standard template to clearly articulate school -wide
  26| expectations for student learning.
  27| As part of the Special Progress Report, the leadership team was charged with addressing the
  28| recommendations that were made by the Commission.  There are four highlighted
  29| recommendations in the Commission‟s report and three additional recommendations identified
  30| through a Special Progress Report.   One of the “completed” recommendations was due to  the
  31| fact that on January 7, 2014 Lunenburg voters supported the design of a grade 6 -12, middle/high
  32| school building project, at a Special Town Meeting.  On January 11, 2014, the town of Lunenburg
  33| voted to approve a ballot proposal to build a new middle/high school.  Voters cast 1,4 14 votes in
  34| favor of the project and only 543 votes were in opposition to the project, in the special election.
  35| The total cost of the project is $72,975,321.  The leadership team reworked the school -wide
  36| learning expectations so that they are aligned with the Common Core State Standards.  The report
  37| was submitted to the Commission on February 4, 2014. Lunenburg High School is currently
  38| working on Core Values and Beliefs about student learning, which will be adopted by LHS once
  39| the process is complete.
  40| SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  41| Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising
  42| and collection endeavors. In the fall, three staff members were involved in a fundraiser to raise
  43| money for the Lunenburg Food Pantry .  Money was collected during lunch and the staff member
  44| who raised the most money, kissed a pig.  Jim Lat tanzi, from Hollis Hills Farm, brought in the
  45| pig for the Thanksgiving rally.  Fun was had by all, and all of the proceeds were donated for the
  46| holiday season.

  32| We were also very pleased to learn that 35 seniors at Lunenburg High School received t he state‟s
  33| John and Abigail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose
  34| MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must
  35| either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English
  36| and Math exams.

   1|                                                                                        Our Mission
   2|                              Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   3|                              knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   4|                                                                                        Our District
   5|                              Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school serving the member towns of:
   6|                                        Ashburnham                                      Harvard                                       Princeton
   7|                                        Ashby                                          Holden                                          Royalston
   8|                                        Athol                                          Hubbardston                                     Sterling
   9|                                         Barre                                          Lumenburg                                      Templeton
  10|                                        Fitchburg                                      Petersham                                      Westminster
  11|                                        Gardner                                        Phillipston                                     Winchendon
  12|                                                                                         Leadership
  13|                              The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  14|                               administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  15|                              contribute to the success of the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the
  16|                              Superintendent and Principal, the team has been able to transform the school into one of the most sought-
  17|                              after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.

  23|                                                                                        Enrollment
  24|                             On June 1, 2014, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,403 students in grades nine through twelve.
  25|                            Students are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (54), Ashby (37), Athol (93),
  26|                            Baire (35), Fitchburg (364), Gardner (158), Harvard (3), Holden (68), Hubbardston (61), Lunenburg (70),
  27|                            Petersham (4), Phillipston (21), Princeton (17), Royalston (22), Sterling (56), Templeton (100), Westminster
  28|                             (T7), and Winchendon (133). The remaining thirty students were from out-of-district towns, including Clinton,
  29|                            Leominster, Natick, Orange, Pepperell, Rutland, Shirley, West Boylston, and Worcester.
  30|                             Throughout 2013-2014, Mouty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community
  31|                             members to leam about and visit the school In October 2013, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders
  32|                            participated in the annual "Tour Day" event Students toured our twenty-vocational/technical areas and
  33|                            learned about the school's challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracuricular
  34|                            programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in the evening with
  35|                            their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with staff members.
  36|                            Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  37|                             admussions. 2013-2014 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for her, as the school recerved 730 applications for admission. Of those, 642 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class
  38|                             The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because
  39|                                                                                               114

===PAGE 115=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                           there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 367 freshmen and 19
   2|                           upperclassmen were admitted.
   3|                           The Vocational Interest Program (VIP.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit
   4|                           Monty Tech after school, and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of
   5|                           vocational/technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2013-2014
   6|                           school year, serving approximately 600 area students.
   7|                                                                                 Class of 2014 Awards
   8|                           Members of the Class of 2014 were awarded approximately $50,000 in scholarships. The Monty Tech
   9|                           Foundation generously provided $34,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $250 to
  10|                           $2,500. The Foundation also awarded $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state
  11|                           organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  12|                           graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates
  13|                           themselves, are grateful for this support.
  14|                           articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Well over 60% of the graduating
  15|                           class of 2014 reported plans to enroll at either a 2-year college, 4-year college/university, or a technical/trade
  16|                           school upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will benefit by
  17|                           saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  18|                                                                                    Financial Report
  19|                           In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2013-2014, a great deal of effort was put forth
  20|                           by the School Committee, administration and staff. The final fiscal year 2013-2014 Educational Plan totaled
  21|                           $24,785,280, which represents a 2.0% increase over the 2012-2013 Educational Plan As a result of changes
  22|                          to the final FY14 State budget and the District's decision not to increase the already-approved community
  23|                           assessments, the District's FY14 budget was approximately $200,000 below the minimum spending required
  24|                           by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70.
  25|                           The District was audited in August 2014 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting fum of
  26|                           Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  27|                                                                                 Grants and Contracts
  28|                           Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many
  29|                           educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2014, state and federal grant sources
  30|                           provided the school with S842,780. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services,
  31|                           Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program
  32|                           Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
  33|                           In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive
  34|                           grant awards totaling $525,000. Included in that total are a number of highly competitive awards, including a
  35|                           $400,000 award from the Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, used to provide
  36|                                                                                              115

   4|                             training opportunities, as the device manifests vital sigus, clinical signs and symptoms. Allocation and

   5|                            walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.

   3|                                                                                    Special Services
   4|                            During the 2013-2014 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
   5|                             pecial services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two hundred students on Individual Education Plans (TEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individualized Sectior
   6|                            504 plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the
   7|                            department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
   8|                            The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated health
   9|                               reening exams, and provides, when necessary, bealth intormation to the special education team for udent's IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full-time school social worker who participates i
  10|                            departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness,
  11|                            maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also fortunate to have on staff a full-time
  12|                            psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three-
  13|                            year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech pathologist, who is available to assist students with
  14|                            disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Chur students also have access to the services of a
  15|                            full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for
  16|                            scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment, as well as crisis intervention.
  17|                            The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the district's Special Education Program, which is
  18|                            reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and
  19|                            evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are
  20|                            used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational
  21|                            Technical School.

  19|                                                                      Posteraduate and Continuing Studies
  20|                            The Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program, also known as Monty Tech Nites, continues to update and
  21|                            add courses that emphasize a commitment to excellence by offering affordable, quality, and enjoyable
  22|                            educational experiences. For the Fall of 2013, Monty Tech offered 101 courses "in-bouse" and another 400+
  23|                            were offered online. Approximately 840 seats were sold for Fall 2013 courses. In comparison, there were 92
  24|                            "in-bouse" courses and, again, over 400 on-line courses offered during the Spring 2014 semester.
  25|                            Approximately 790 seats were sold for Spring 2013 courses.
  26|                            In March 2014 the postgraduate program successfully graduated our third class of fifteen Emergency Medical
  27|                            Technicians. The students took their practical exam at Monty Tech and proceeded to take their written exam at
  28|                            a state-designated facility. Several graduates have already found employment in their chosen field.
  29|                            The Director of the Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program actively seeks information to develop new
  30|                            (and expand existing) certificate and licensure programs that align with regional workforce needs and
  31|                            employment trends.
  32|                                                                             Practical Nursing Program
  33|                            The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a caring
  34|                            manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in bealth status in diverse health care settings.
  35|                            This mission, which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Montachusett Regional Vocational
  36|                            Technical School District, accomplished the following:
  37|                                  • Identfies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  38|                                  • Stresses the importance of developing critical thinking skills to function safely, effectively, and
  39|                                     productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  40|                                 • Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment
  41|                                                                                              126

   1|                            In June 2014, thirty-three (33) students graduated and entered the mursing profession. The class achieved an
   2|                            initial NCLEX pass rate of 88%, and just over 91% of the 2014 graduates are currently working in the North
   3|                            Central area as LPNs in various health care settings from long term care, sub-acute care, clinics and prison
   4|                            health care.
   5|                            The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to develop the "LPN to BSN Bridge" relationship with
   6|                            Fitchburg State University. Additionally, the program is proud to report an expanded partnership with
   7|                            Fitchburg State University, as both institutions were recently awarded a grant known as the "Nurse of the
   8|                            Future" initiative, awarded by the state Department of Higher Education The grant funding will assist
   9|                            veterans hoping to transition from military to civilian healthcare fields, granting academic credits for previous
  10|                            military training and experience in related fields. The goal of this unique partnership is to have five students
  11|                            enrolled in the program at both Monty Tech and Fitchburg State University annually.

  17|                            there are a mumber of programs and initiatives that we expect will have a positive impact on our school and
  18|                            students for years to come.

  20|                            area colleges, Monty Tech hopes to bring additional vocational-technical training opportunities to students that
  21|                            may result in articulated credits. Providing students with an opportunity to earn college credits while still in
  22|                            high school will not only save the students time and money, it validates they very rigorous educational
  23|                            programs in place here at Monty Tech. In 2014-2015, school officials hope to work closely with area college
  24|                            leaders to also accomplish the following:

   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| SPECIAL TOWN MEETING JANUARY 7, 2014
   4| The Moderator declared a quorum present and called the meeting to order at 7:02 PM in the Lunenburg
   5| High School Auditorium. Due to the large turnout a recess was called to allow voters to check in. Meeting
   6| resumed at 7:26 p.m. The Moderator led us in the Pledge of Allegiance and reminded attendees of the
   7| rules of procedure.
   8| ARTICLE 1.  To see if the Town will vote to appropriate, borrow or transfer from available funds, a sum
   9| of money to be expended under the direction of the School Building Committee for construction of a new
  10| Lunenburg Middle/High School, designed for approximately 820 students in Grades 6 – 12, to be located
  11| at 1079 Massachusetts Avenue, Lunenburg, MA 01462, which school facili ty shall have an anticipated
  12| useful life as an educational facility for the instruction of school children of at least 50 years, and for
  13| which the Town may be eligible for a school construction grant from the Massachusetts School Building
  14| Authority (“MSBA”). The MSBA‟s grant program is a non -entitlement, discretionary program based on
  15| need, as determined by the MSBA, and any project costs the Town incurs in excess of any grant approved
  16| by and received from the MSBA shall be the sole responsibility of the To wn. Any grant that the Town of
  17| Lunenburg may receive from the MSBA for the Project shall not exceed the lesser of (1) fifty -nine point
  18| eleven percent (59.11%) of eligible , approved project costs, as determined by the MSBA, or (2) the total
  19| maximum grant a mount determined by the MSBA. Board of Selectmen recommends approval, 5 -0.
  20| Finance Committee recommends approval, 5 -1. VOTED (2/3rds declare d) to appropriate by borrowing
  21| $72,975,321.00 and to meet this appropriation the Board of Selectmen and Town Treasurer are authorized
  22| to borrow said sum under M.G.L. chapter 44, or pursuant to any other enabling authority. The Town  of
  23| Lunenburg acknowledges that the MSBA‟s grant program is a non -entitlement, discretionary program
  24| based on need, as determined by the MSBA , and any project costs the Town of Lunenburg incurs in
  25| excess of any grant approved by and received from the MSBA shall be the sole responsibility of the Town
  26| of Lunenburg; provided further that any grant that the Town of Lunenburg may receive from the MS BA
  27| for the Project shall not exceed the lesser of (1) fifty -nine point eleven percent (59.11%) of eligible,
  28| approved project costs, as determined by the MSBA, or (2) the total maximum grant amount determined
  29| by the MSBA; provided that any appropriation her eunder shall be subject to and contingent upon an
  30| affirmative vote of the Town to exempt the amounts required for the payment of interest and principal on
  31| said borrowing from the limitations on taxes imposed by M.G.L. 59, Section 21C (Proposition 2 ½); and
  32| that the amount of borrowing authorized pursuant to this vote shall be reduced by any grant amount set
  33| forth in the Project Funding Agreement that may be executed between the Town of Lunenburg and the
  34| MSBA.
  35| ARTICLE 2.  To see if the Town will vote to ame nd the Town‟s Zoning Bylaw by adding a new Section
  36| 9.0, entitled T emporary Moratorium on Medical M arijuana Treatment Centers/Registered Marijuana
  37| Dispensaries, that would provide as follows, and further to amen d the Table of Contents to add Section
  38| 9.0, “T emporary Moratorium on Medical Marijuana Treatment Centers/Registered Marijuana
  39| Dispensaries:”
  40| Section 9.0.1. Purpose
  41| By vote at the State election on November 6, 2012, the voters of the commonwealth approved a law
  42| regulating the cultivation, distribution, possession and use of marijuana for medical purposes. The law
  43| provides that it is effective on January 1, 2013.  On May 8, 2013, the State Department of Public Health
  44| promulgated Regulations that became effective on May 24, 2013. Currently under the Zoning Bylaw, a
  45| Medical Marijuana Treatment Center, identified in the State Regulations as a Registered Marijuana
  46| Dispensary, is not a permitted use in the Town of Lunenburg.  The State Regulations provide guidance to
  47| the Town in regulating medical marijuana, including Medical Marijuana Treatment Centers/Registered

===PAGE 134=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 134
   2| Dispensary, is not a permitted use in the Town of Lunenburg.  The State Regulations provide
   3| guidance to the Town in regulating medical marijuan a, including Medical Marijuana Treatment
   4| Centers/Registered Marijuana Dispensaries.  The regulation of medical marijuana raises novel
   5| and complex legal, planning, and public safety issues and the Town needs time to study and
   6| consider the regulation of such  use and address such novel and complex issues, as well as to
   7| address the potential impact of the State regulations on local zoning and to undertake a planning
   8| process to consider amending the Zoning Bylaw regarding regulation of medical marijuana
   9| treatment centers and other uses related to the regulation of medical marijuana.  The Town
  10| intends to adopt a temporary moratorium on the use of land and structures in the Town for
  11| Medical Marijuana Treatment Center/Registered Marijuana Dispensaries so as to allow the Town
  12| sufficient time to engage in a planning process to address the effects of such structures and uses
  13| in the Town and to enact bylaws in a manner consistent with sound land use planning goals and
  14| objectives.
  15| Section 9.0.2. Definition
  16| “Medical Mari juana Treatment Center” shall mean a not -for-profit entity, as defined by
  17| Massachusetts law only, registered by the Department of Public Health as a Registered
  18| Marijuana Dispensary, that acquires, cultivates, possesses, processes (including development of
  19| related products such as food, tinctures, aerosols, oils or ointments), transfers, transports, sells,
  20| distributes, dispenses, or administers marijuana, products containing marijuana, related supplies,
  21| or educational materials to qualifying patients or their personal caregivers.
  22| Section 9.0.3. Temporary Moratorium
  23| For the reasons set forth above and notwithstanding any other provisions of the Zoning B ylaw to
  24| the contrary, the Town hereby adopts a temporary moratorium on the use of land or structures for
  25| a Medical Marijuana Treatment Center/Registered Marijuana Dispensary.  The moratorium shall
  26| be in effect through December 31, 2014 or until such time as the Town adopts Zoning Bylaw
  27| amendments that regulate Medical Marijuana Treatment Centers/Registered Mari juana
  28| Dispensaries and related uses, whichever occurs earlier.  During the moratorium period, the
  29| Town shall undertake a planning process to address the potential impacts of medical marijuana in
  30| the Town, consider the State Regulations and related uses, an d shall consider adopting new
  31| Zoning Bylaws to address the impact and operation of Medical Marijuana Treatment
  32| Centers/Registered Marijuana Dispensaries and related uses;  or take any action relative thereto.
  33| Board of Selectmen recommends disapproval.  Fi nance Committee – no recommendation.
  34| Planning Board recommends approval 5-1.  ARTICLE 2 was DEFEATED
  35| Special Town Meeting adjourned at 9:30 P.M.

  29| Access Cable Committee, by deleting the existing bylaw and replacing it with the followin g; or take any
  30| other action relative thereto.  [Submitted by the Board of Selectmen]  Board of Selectmen recommends

  32| Article 1.  Establishment
  33| The name of the organization shall  be the Public Access Cable Committee, herein referred to as PACC.
  34| The Selectmen activated and appointed the PACC in July 2007 to assist with the development of
  35| Lunenburg TV Channels 8 & 9.
  36| Article 2.  Mission Statement
  37| The Committee was created for the purpose of establishing a local access TV Station operating on cable
  38| for the Town of Lunenburg.  PACC is to oversee programming in areas of public affairs, education,
  39| entertainment and town government.  The PACC shall be responsible for the formulation and
  40| promulgation of policy directives and guidelines for the operation of the PACC; provided, however, that

===PAGE 144=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 144
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| nothing in this section shall be construed to authorize any member of the PACC, nor a majority of such
   5| members, to become involved in the day-to-day administration of the Lunenburg Public Access.
   6| Article 3.  Section intentionally deleted.
   7| Article 4.  Membership
   8| Section 1.  Number of Members.  PACC shall consist of  five- (5) members who shall be appointed by the
   9| Lunenburg Board of Selectmen from residents  of the Town of Lunenburg or full time students at
  10| Lunenburg High School.
  11| Section 2.  Term of Appointment.  The term shall be for three -(3) years or, in the case of vacancy, any
  12| portion of an unexpired term, so arranged that the term of office as nearly an equal number of members as
  13| is possible shall expire each year.  The above Term of Appointment shall be effective with the
  14| appointments by the Board of Selectmen commencing in fiscal year 2009.
  15| Section 4.  Composition of Membership.  Members shall reflect the makeup of the community at large.
  16| Any resident of the town or any Lunenburg High School student is eligible to submit his/ her name to the
  17| Lunenburg Selectmen for consideration to be appointed to PACC.
  18| Section 5.  Voting Power.  All duly appointed cur rent members of PACC shall have the right to vote on
  19| all issues.
  20| Section 6.  Resignation.  Members appointed by the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen shall resign in
  21| writing to the Board of Selectmen and PACC.
  22| Section 7.  Attendance.  Regular meeting attendance  is expected for all members of PACC.  In the event
  23| of three- (3) consecutive absences, except for the reason of health or extenuating circumstances as duly
  24| reported to the Chair in advance of the meeting, PACC may request resignation of that member throug h
  25| the appointing authority.
  26| Section 8.  Vacancies.  Vacancies shall be filled by the Board of Selectmen.
  27| Section 9.  Representations.  No member of PACC  shall make written or oral presentations for PACC
  28| unless authorized by a vote of PACC.
  29| Section 10.  No n-PACC Members.  PACC may appoint a Non -PACC member to act on PACC‟s behalf.
  30| In such a case, all written materials, oral presentations or communications from the Non -PACC member
  31| on behalf of PACC, will need to be approved by a vote of PACC prior to his/ h er  presentation or
  32| execution.
  33| Article 5.  Meetings
  34| Section 1.  Regular Meetings.  Regular meetings will be held as determined by the PACC with the time
  35| and place to be posted at Town Hall.

===PAGE 145=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 145
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| Section 2.  Special Meetings.  The Chair or a majority of PACC members may call for Special Meetings.
   5| Section 3.  Organizational Meeting.  The organizational meeting shall be held at the regularly scheduled
   6| monthly meeting after the start of each fiscal year.
   7| Section 4.  Open Meeting Law.  All meetings shall be posted an d conducted in compliance with the
   8| provisions of Chapter 39, Sections 23, 23A and 23B.  The meeting agenda shall be posted in accordance
   9| with the Massachusetts Open Meetings Law and all other applicable Town Bylaws.
  10| Section 5.  Quorum.  The presence of a s imple majority of the total membership, as defined in Article 4,
  11| Section 1 shall be necessary and sufficient to constitute a quorum.
  12| Article 6.  Officers.
  13| Section 1.  Officers.  The officers shall consist of a Chairman, Vice Chairman and Secretary as elect ed by
  14| the members.
  15| Section 2.  Election.  Election for officers shall take place at the PACC organizational meeting.
  16| Section 3.  Terms of Office.  Term of office for the officers shall be for one - (1) year, with no restrictions
  17| on re-election.
  18| Section 4.  Vacancy.  Any vacancy may be filled at a regular or special meeting of PACC.  It shall be for
  19| the unexpired term of the previous incumbent officer.  In the case of the Chair, the Vice -Chair shall fill
  20| the position of the Chair for the remainder of the unexpired term of the Chair.
  21| Section 5.  Duties of Officers.  Chair- the Chair shall be the chief executive officer and be subject to the
  22| direction of PACC.  The Chair or his/ her designee shall provide the agendas for meetings,  preside at
  23| meeting and orient new members.  The Chair or his/ her designee shall provide all PACC members a copy
  24| of the Open Meeting Law as required by state statute.  Vice-Chair- the Vice-Chair shall exercise all the
  25| functions of the Chair, during the absence or disability of the Chair, and when so acting, shall have all the
  26| powers and be subject to all restrictions of the Chair.  Secretary- the Secretary shall take the meeting
  27| minutes, post notices of upcoming meetings and provide minutes to PACC members.  He/ She shall post
  28| the meeting agenda in accordance with the Massachusetts Open Meetings Law and all other applicable
  29| Town Bylaws.
  30| Article 7.  Amendments.  The PACC may consider amendments or alterations to these by-Laws at either a
  31| regular or special PACC meeting.  In either case, n otification of the meeting and its purpose shall be
  32| given fourteen- (14) days prior to the assembly.  The notice shall include a summary of the proposed
  33| amendment, the full text of the proposed amendment or alteration, and a statement of the purpose of the
  34| proposed change.  The proposed amendment must be approved by a majority vote of PACC.  It will then
  35| be sent to the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen for approval, and if so approved, for insertion in the warrant
  36| and be submitted to the next Town Meeting for ap proval.  This protocol applies only to the PACC‟s
  37| ability to initiate changes to these bylaws, and is not intended to usurp the rights given to registered voters
  38| of the Town specified in MGL Chapter 39, Section 10.

  18| ARTICLE  27.   To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw, Section 4.0, Use Regulations,
  19| by adding a new Section 4.16 entitled Registered Marijuana Dispensaries, or take any action relative
  20| thereto.  [Submitted by the Planning Board]  Board of Select men recommend approval. Finance
  21| Committee no direct financial impact.
  22| 4.16.1. Purpose
  23| To provide for the placement of Registered Marijuana Dispensary (RMDs), in accordance with the
  24| Humanitarian Medical Use of Marijuana Act, G.L. c. 94C App. 1 -1 – Chapter 369 of the Acts of 2012 in
  25| locations suitable for lawful medical marijuana facilities and to minimize adve rse impact of RMDs on
  26| adjacent properties, residential neighborhoods, historic districts, schools, playgrounds and other locations
  27| where minors congregate by regulating the siting, design, placement, security and removal of RMDs.
  28| 4.16.2.  Applicability
  29| 4.16.2.1. All RMDs shall comply with The Regulations promulgated by the Massachusetts Department of
  30| Public Health (DPH) 105 CMR 725.000 Implementation of an Act for the Humanitarian Medical Use of
  31| Marijuana, effective May 24, 2013, and any subsequent amendments thereto.
  32| 4.16.2.2. The Town reserves the right to require provisions in the DPH Regulations for which the state
  33| granted waivers and/or exemptions to the RMD applicant based on the provisions of Section 8.3.3. (as to
  34| special permit) and Section 8.4. (as to development plan review).

===PAGE 147=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 147
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| 4.16.3. Definition
   5| Registered Marijuana Dispensary: also known as a Medical Marijuana Treatment Center, means a not-for-
   6| profit entity registered under 105 CMR 725.000, that acquires, cultivates, possesses, processes (includin g
   7| development of related products such as edible marijuana -infused products, tinctures, aerosols, oils, or
   8| ointments), transfers, transports, sells, distributes, dispenses, or administers marijuana, products
   9| containing marijuana, related supplies, or educa tional materials to registered qualifying patients or their
  10| personal caregivers.  Unless otherwise specified, RMD refers to the site(s) of dispensing, cultivation, and
  11| preparation of marijuana.
  12| 4.16.4. Location
  13| 4.16.4.1. RMD‟s that include retail, processing and cultivation are allowed in Commercial Districts
  14| (C) by special permit.
  15| 4.16.4.2. Cultivation and/or agricultural processing:  Allowed in all Districts by right if applicant is
  16| eligible for protection under Section 4.1.3(b) and G.L. c. 40A, § 3. Any cultivation within a building will
  17| require a special permit.
  18| 4.16.4.3. RMD facilities that cultivate and process, but do not do retail of marijuana, are allowed in
  19| Office Park and Industrial Districts (OP/I) by special permit.
  20| Section 4.16.5. Procedure for Submittal
  21| 4.16.5.1. The Planning Board shall be the Special Permit Granting Authority (SPGA) for a RMD Special
  22| Permit per MA Gen. Law 40A, Section 9. Siting shall be by Special Permit and Development Plan
  23| Review per Section 8.4 of the Lunenburg Protective Bylaw.
  24| 4.16.5.1.a) Criteria  In granting any special permit, the Planning Board shall assure that the proposed
  25| use:
  26|   1) Will not be injurious or dangerous to the public health or unduly hazardous
  27| because of traffic congestion, danger or fire or explosion or other reasons.
  28|   2) Will not have a material adverse effect on the value of land and buildings in the
  29| neighborhood or on the amenities of the neighborhood.
  30|   3) Will be operated with reasonable regard for order and sightliness, if an open use.
  31|   4) Will not produce noise, vibration, smoke, dust, odor, heat or glare observable at
  32| the lot lines in amounts clearly detrimental to the normal use of adjacent property.
  33| 4.16.5.2. It is recommended that a concept plan showing the proposed use be submitted to th e Planning
  34| Board for review and discussion prior to the filing for Development Plan Review.
  35| 4.16.5.3. The provisions of Section 8.3.3. (as to Special Permits) and Section 8.4. (as to Development
  36| Plan Review) shall apply.

===PAGE 148=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 148
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| 4.16.5.4. Filing under Section 8.3.3. and 8.4. may be simultaneous.
   5| 4.16.5.5. A special permit granted under this Section shall have a term limited to the duration of the
   6| applicant‟s ownership of the premises as a RMD.  A special permit may be transferred only with the
   7| approval of the Special Permit Granting Authority in the form of an amendment to the special permit with
   8| all information required in this Section.
   9| 4.16.6. Conditional Standards
  10| 4.16.6.1 Distance All proposed RMDs shall be sited accordingly as stated in Massachusetts Department
  11| of Public Health (DPH) 105 CMR 725.110 (A) (14), which states a RMD shall not be sited within a
  12| radius of five hundred (500) feet of a school, daycare center, or any facility in which children commonly
  13| congregate. The 500 foot distance under this section is measured in a straight line from the nearest point
  14| of the facility in question to the nearest point of the proposed RMD.
  15| 4.16.6.2 Setbacks and Buffer Strips  Cultivation facilities located outside of Retail Commercial (RC),
  16| Commercial (C), or Office Park and Industrial Districts (OP/I) shall be surrounded by a buffer strip which
  17| shall be two hundred (200) feet in depth unless the applicant can demonstrate, and the Planning Board
  18| finds, that adequate buffering can be provided in a narrower buffer strip.  I n all other districts existing
  19| setbacks will apply.
  20| 4.16.6.3 Design Standards In addition to requirements contained elsewhere in this Bylaw, Section 4.6.5.
  21| Design Standards shall be applicable to a RMD.  The applicant shall meet all requirements of the
  22| Commercial District (C) or DPH guidelines, whichever is more restrictive.
  23| 4.16.6.4  Waivers   The Planning Board may waive any of the conditional standards within this section
  24| provided that such waiver will not derogate from the intent established herein.
  25| 4.16.7.  Security
  26| 4.16.9.1.  All security measures to be approved by the Lunenburg Fire and Police Chiefs.  An active
  27| security system shall be required for all RMD locations and approved by both the Lunenburg Fire and
  28| Police Chiefs and submitted to the Planning Board
  29| 4.16.9.2.  RMDs shall be open to inspection by the Fire Department, Police Department, Building Official
  30| and the Board of Health with twenty -four (24) hours‟ notice of request for such inspection to be made by
  31| the Town Department or Official.  A pr operty contact shall be available to such Town Department or
  32| Official twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week.
  33| 4.16.10.  Documentation
  34| The Planning Board shall be provided with all decisions or approvals, denials or other substantive actions
  35| by DPH regarding the RMD and all submittals of information between the applicant or RMD and DPH.
  36| VOTED UNANIMOUSLY

===PAGE 149=== (plain, 34 lines)
   1| 149
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| ARTICLE  28.   To see if the Town will vote to amend the General Bylaws by inserting a new Section
   5| XXXI, entitled Right to Farm Bylaw, or take any acti on relative thereto.  [Submitted by the Agricultural
   6| Commission]  Board of Selectmen recommend approval 5 -0.  Finance Committee no direct financial
   7| impact.
   8| Section 1.  Legislative Purpose and Intent.  The purpose and intent of the Bylaw is to state with e mphasis
   9| the Right to Farm accorded to all citizens of the Commonwealth under Article 97, of the Constitution, and
  10| all state statutes and regulations thereunder including but not limited to Massachusetts General Laws,
  11| Chapter 40A, Section 3, Paragraph 1; Ch apter 90, Section 9; Chapter 11, Section 125A and Chapter 128,
  12| Section 1A.  We the citizens of Lunenburg restate and republish these rights pursuant to the Town‟s
  13| authority conferred by Article 89 of the Articles of Amendment of the Massachusetts Constitut ion,
  14| (“Home Rule Amendment”).  This General Bylaw encourages the pursuit of agriculture, promotes
  15| agriculture-based economic opportunities, and protects farmlands within the Town of Lunenburg by
  16| allowing agricultural uses and related activities to function  with minimal conflict with abutters and local
  17| agencies.  This Bylaw shall apply to all jurisdictional areas within the Town.
  18| Section 2.  Definitions .  The word “farm” shall include any parcel or contiguous parcel of land, or water
  19| bodies used for the prim ary purpose of agriculture, or accessory thereto.  The words “farming” or
  20| “agriculture” or their derivatives shall include, but not be limited to the following:
  21| a. Farming in all its branches and the cultivation and tillage of the soil
  22| b. Dairying
  23| c. Production, cu ltivation, growing and harvesting of any agricultural, aquacultural, floricultural,
  24| viticultural or horticultural commodities
  25| d. Growing and harvesting of forest products upon forest land and any other forestry or lumbering
  26| operations
  27| e. Keeping and raising of l ivestock, horses, poultry, swine, cattle, sheep ratites (such as emus,
  28| ostriches, rheas) and camelids (such as llamas and camels) and other domesticated animals for
  29| food and other agricultural purpose, including bees and fur-bearing animals.
  30| “Farming” shall encompass activities including, but not limited to the following:
  31| Operation and Transportation of slow-moving farm equipment over roads within the Town;
  32| Control of pests, including, but not limited to, insects, weeds, predators and disease organisms of
  33| plants and animals;
  34| Application of manure, fertilizers and pesticides;

===PAGE 150=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 150
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| Conducting agricultural related educational and farm based recreational activities, including agri -
   5| tourism, provided that the activities are related to marketing the agricultural output or services of the
   6| farm;
   7| Processing and packaging of the agricultural output of the farm and the operations of a Farmer‟s
   8| Market or Farm Stand including signage thereto;
   9| Maintenance, repair, or storage of seasonal equipment or apparatus owned or leased by the farm
  10| owner or manager used expressly for the purpose of propagation, processing, management or sale of
  11| the agricultural products; and
  12| On farm relocation of earth and the clearing of ground for farming operations.
  13| Section 3.  Right -to-Farm Declaration.  The Right-to-Farm is hereby recognized to exist within the
  14| town of Lunenburg.  The above described agricultural activities may occur (of)on holidays, weekdays
  15| and weekends by night or day and shall include the incidental noise, odors, dust and fumes associated
  16| with normally accepted agricultural practices.  It is hereby determined that whatever impact may be
  17| caused to others through the normal practice of agriculture is more than offset by the benefits of
  18| farming to the neighborhood, community and society in general.  The benefits and protections of the
  19| Bylaw are intended to apply (exclusively) to those (commercial) agricultural and farming operations
  20| and activities conducted in accordance with generally accepted agricultural practices.  Moreover,
  21| nothing in this Right-to-Farm Bylaw shall be deemed as acquiring any interest in land, or as imposing
  22| any land use regulation, which is properly the subject of state statute, regulation or local zoning law.
  23| Section 4.  Disclosure Notification .  In order to ensure t hat prospective owners and prospective
  24| tenants are aware of the policy of the Town of Lunenburg expressed in this Bylaw regarding
  25| agricultural uses and practices, the following notification of this policy shall prominently posted in
  26| the Town Hall within 30  days of this Bylaw becoming effective.  Copies will also be made available
  27| for distribution.  “This notification is to inform persons and entities who are about to acquire or lease
  28| rental property within the Town of Lunenburg that it is the policy of the Town of Lunenburg to
  29| conserve, protect and encourage the maintenance and improvement of the agricultural land, for the
  30| production of food and other agricultural products and for the preservation of its natural and
  31| ecological value.  This notification is to  further inform such owners and entities that farming
  32| activities, including the raising and keeping of livestock, take place in the Town of Lunenburg and
  33| that such activities may cause or create noise, (duct) dust and odors which adversely impact or are
  34| incompatible with the use or enjoyment of the property within the Town, including the property about
  35| to be acquired or leased.”
  36| Property owners should make efforts to inform prospective tenants or buyers that Lunenburg is a
  37| Right-to-Farm community.  In addition, the notification language required by this section shall appear
  38| annually in the Town‟s Annual Report.
  39| Section 5.  Resolution of Disputes .  Any person who seeks to complain about the operation of a farm
  40| may, notwithstanding pursing any other available remedy, file a grievance with the Select Board, the

===PAGE 151=== (plain, 30 lines)
   1| 151
   2| TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS:
   3| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING MAY 3, 2014
   4| Zoning Enforcement Officer, or the Board of Health, depending on the nature of the grievance.  The
   5| filing of the grievance does not suspend the time within which to pursue any other available remedies
   6| that the aggrieved may have.  The Zoning Enforcement Officer or Select Board shall forward a copy
   7| of the grievance to the Agricultural Commission or its agent, which shall review and facilitate the
   8| resolution of the grievance, and report its recommendations t o the referring Town authority within an
   9| agreed upon time frame.  The Board of Health, except in case of imminent danger or public health
  10| risk, may forward a copy of the grievance to the Agricultural Commission or its agent, which shall
  11| review and facilitate the resolution of the grievance, and report its recommendations to the Board of
  12| Health within an agreed upon time frame.
  13| Section 6.  Severability Clause.  If any part of the Bylaw is for any reason held to be unconstitutional
  14| or invalid, such decision shall not affect the remainder of the Bylaw.  The Town of Lunenburg hereby
  15| declares the provisions of this Bylaw to be severable.   VOTED UNANIMOUSLY (with amendments
  16| as noted with strikethroughs and italics.)
