   2| THE 2014 BALLOT QUESTIONS
   3| QUESTION 1: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
   4| This proposed law would eliminate the requirement that the state’s gasoline tax, which was 24 cents per gallon as of September
   5| 2013, (1) be adjusted every year by the percentage change in the Consumer Price Index over the preceding year,  but  (2) not be
   6| adjusted below 21.5 cents per gallon.
   7| WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
   8| A YES VOTE would eliminate the requirement that the state’s gas tax be adjusted annually based on the Consum er Price Index.
   9| A NO VOTE would make no change in the laws regarding the gas tax.
  10|  PREC A PREC  B PREC C PREC D TOTAL
  11| YES 698 539 635 626 2498
  12| NO 456 395 399 401 1651
  13| BLANKS 33 21 19 25 98
  14| TOTAL 1187 955 1053 1052                             4247
  15| QUESTION 2: Law Proposed by Initiative Petition
  16| This proposed law would expand the state’s beverage container deposit law, also known as the Bottle Bill, to require deposits  on
  17| containers for all non-alcoholic non-carbonated drinks in liquid form intended for human consumption, except beverages primarily
  18| derived from dairy products, infant formula, and FDA approved medicines.  The proposed law would not cover containers made
  19| of paper-based biodegradable material and aseptic multi-material packages such as juice boxes or pouches.  The proposed law
  20| would require the state Secretary of Energy and Environmental Affairs (EEA) to adjust the container deposit amount every  five
  21| years to reflect (to the nearest whole cent) changes in the consumer price index, but the value could not be set below five cents.
  22| The proposed law would increase the minimum handling fee that beverage distributors must pay dealers for each properly
  23| returned empty beverage container, which was 21/4 cents as of September 2013, to 31/2 cents.  I t would also increase the
  24| minimum handling fee that bottlers must pay distributors and dealers for each properly returned reusable beverage container,
  25| which was 1 cent as of September 2013, to 31/2 cents.  The Secretary of EEA would review the fee amounts every five years
  26| and make appropriate adjustments to reflect changes in the consumer price index as well as changes in the costs incurred by
  27| redemption centers.  The proposed law defines a redemption center as any business whose primary purpose is the rede mption
  28| of beverage containers and that is not ancillary to any other business.  The proposed law would direct the Secretary of EEA t o
  29| issue regulations allowing small dealers to seek exemptions from accepting empty deposit containers.  The proposed law wou ld
  30| define small dealers as any person or business, including the operator of a vending machine, who sells beverages in beverage
  31| containers to consumers, with a contiguous retail space of 3,000 square feet or less, excluding office and stock room space;  and
  32| fewer than four locations under the same ownership in the Commonwealth.  The proposed law would require that the regulations
  33| consider at least the health, safety, and convenience of the public, including the distribution of dealers and redemption cen ters by
  34| population or by distance or both.  The proposed law would set up a state Clean Environment Fund to receive certain unclaimed
  35| container deposits.  The Fund would be used, subject to appropriation by the State Legislature, to support programs such as t he
  36| proper management of solid waste, water resource protection, parkland, urban forestry,  air quality and climate protection.  The
  37| proposed law would allow a dealer, distributor, redemption center or bottler to refuse to accept any beverage container that is not
  38| marked as being refundable in Massachusetts.  The proposed law would take effect on April 22, 2015.
  39| WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
  40| A YES VOTE would expand the state’s beverage container deposit law to require deposits on containers for all non -alcoholic,
  41| non-carbonated drinks with certain exceptions, increase the associated handling fees, and make other changes to the law.
  42| A NO VOTE would make no change in the laws regarding beverage container deposits.
  43|  PREC A PREC  B PREC C PREC D TOTAL
  44| YES 208 152 176 198 732
  45| NO 969 792 872 848 3481
  46| BLANKS 10 13 5 6 34
  47| TOTAL 1187 955 1053 1052 4247

===PAGE 162=== (plain, 51 lines)
   1| 162
   2| QUESTION 3: LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
   3|  This proposed law would (1) prohibit the Massachusetts Gaming Commission from issuing any license for a casino or other
   4| gaming establishment with table games and slot machines, or any license for a gaming establishment with slot machines:  (2)
   5| prohibit any such casino or slots gaming under any such licenses that the Commission might have issued before the proposed
   6| law took effect; and (3) prohibit wagering on the simulcasting of live greyhound races.  The proposed law would change the
   7| definition of “illegal gaming” under Massachusetts law to include wagering on the simulcasting of live greyhound races, as we ll as
   8| table games and slot machines at Commission-licensed casinos, and slot machines at other Commission-licensed gaming
   9| establishments.  This law would make those types of gaming subject to existing state laws providing criminal penalties for, o r
  10| otherwise regulating or prohibiting, activities involving illegal gaming.  The proposed law states that if any of its parts were
  11| declared invalid, the other parts would stay in effect.
  12| WHAT YOUR VOTE WILL DO
  13| A YES VOTE would prohibit casinos, any gaming establishment with slot machines, and wagering on simulcast greyhound
  14| races.
  15| A NO VOTE would make no change in the current laws regarding gaming.
  16|  PREC A PREC  B PREC C PREC D TOTAL

  19| BLANKS 16 23 11 14 64
  20| TOTAL 1187 955 1053 1052 4247
  21| QUESTION 4: LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
  22| This proposed law would entitle employees in Massachusetts to earn and use sick time according to certain conditions.
  23| Employees who work for employers having eleven or more employees could earn and u se up to 40 hours of paid sick time per
  24| calendar year, while employees working for smaller employers could earn and use up to 40 hours of unpaid sick time per
  25| calendar year. An employee could use earned sick time if required to miss work in order (1) to ca re for a physical or mental
  26| illness, injury or medical condition affecting the employee or the employee’s child, spouse, parent, or parent of a spouse; ( 2) to
  27| attend routine medical appointments of the employee or the employee’s child, spouse, parent, or p arent of a spouse; or (3) to
  28| address the effects of domestic violence on the employee or the employee’s dependent child. Employees would earn one hour of
  29| sick time for every 30 hours worked, and would begin accruing those hours on the date of hire or on Ju ly 1, 2015, whichever is
  30| later. Employees could begin to use earned sick time on the 90 th day after hire. The proposed law would cover both private and
  31| public employers, except that employees of a particular city or town would be covered only if, as requir ed by the state
  32| constitution, the proposed law were made applicable by local or state legislative vote or by appropriation of sufficient fund s to pay
  33| for the benefit. Earned paid sick time would be compensated at the same hourly rate paid to the employee w hen the sick time is
  34| used. Employees could carry over up to 40 hours of unused sick time to the next calendar year, but could not use more than 40
  35| hours in a calendar year. Employers would not have to pay employees for unused sick time at the end of their employment. If an
  36| employee missed work for a reason eligible for earned sick time, but agreed with the employer to work the same number of
  37| hours or shifts in the same or next pay period, the employee would not have to use earned sick time for the missed ti me, and the
  38| employer would not have to pay for that missed time. Employers would be prohibited from requiring such an employee to work
  39| additional hours to make up for missed time, or to find a replacement employee. Employers could require certification of the need
  40| for sick time if an employee used sick time for more than 24 consecutively scheduled work hours. Employers could not delay th e
  41| taking of or payment for earned sick time because they have not received the certification. Employees would have to make  a
  42| good faith effort to notify the employer in advance if the need for earned sick time is foreseeable. Employers would be prohi bited
  43| from interfering with or retaliating based on an employee’s exercise of earned sick time rights, and from retaliating base d on an
  44| employee’s support of another employee’s exercise of such rights. The proposed law would not override employers’ obligations
  45| under any contract or benefit plan with more generous provisions than those in the proposed law. Employers that have their own
  46| policies providing as much paid time off, usable for the same purposes and under the same conditions, as the proposed law
  47| would not be required to provide additional paid sick time. The Attorney General would enforce the proposed law, using the sa me
  48| enforcement procedures applicable to other state wage laws, and employees could file suits in court to enforce their earned sick
  49| time rights. The Attorney General would have to prepare a multilingual notice regarding the right to earned sick time, and
  50| employers would be required to post the notice in a conspicuous location and to provide a copy to employees. The state
  51| Executive Office of Health and Human Services, in consultation with the Attorney General, would develop a multilingual outrea ch

===PAGE 163=== (plain, 28 lines)
   1| 163
   2| program to inform the public of the availability of earned sick time. The proposed law would take effect on July 1, 2015, and
   3| states that if any of its parts were declared invalid, the other parts would stay in effect.
   4| A YES VOTE would entitle employees in Massachusetts to earn and use sick time according to certain conditions.
   5| A NO VOTE would make no change in the laws regarding earned sick time.

   7| YES 581 484 485 504 2054
   8| NO 581 444 555 530 2110
   9| BLANKS 25 27 13 18 83
  10| TOTAL 1187 955 1053 1052 4247
